Bounds on photon scattering
Kelian Häring, Aditya Hebbar, Denis Karateev, Marco Meineri, João Penedones

Abstract
We study 2-to-2 scattering amplitudes of massless spin one particles in d = 4 d=4 space-time dimensions, like real world photons. We define a set of non-perturbative observables (Wilson coefficients) which describe these amplitudes at low energies. We use full non-linear unitarity to construct various novel numerical bounds on these observables. For completeness, we also rederive some bounds using positivity only. We discover and explain why some of these Wilson coefficients cannot be bounded.



CERN-TH-2022-161

1

For an overview of recent results and discussion of some future directions, see
Kruczenski:2022lot
.

In this paper we focus on
4
space-time dimensions.
We consider a class of theories
which have a single photon-like particle, namely a massless particle with spin one. We denote this particle by
\gamma
.
The goal of this paper is to study the 2-to-2 scattering amplitudes of the process

\gamma(\lambda_{1})\gamma(\lambda_{2})\rightarrow\gamma(\lambda_{3})\gamma(\lambda_{4})\,,

(1)

where
\lambda_{i}
is the helicity of the in/out particles.
We are agnostic about the high energy behaviour of the theory apart from the requirement that the 2-to-2 scattering amplitude must obey the usual S-matrix bootstrap principles: Lorentz invariance, unitarity and analyticity.

Scattering amplitudes of particles with spin in
4d
was reviewed systematically in
Hebbar:2020ukp
.
2
2

2

This problem was studied in the 60s by many authors
Jacob:1959at
;
Trueman:1964zzb
;
Hara:1970gc
;
Hara:1971kj
, see the older review
Martin:102663
for a more comprehensive list. See also
Bellazzini:2016xrt
;
deRham:2017zjm
for more recent discussions.

We will use their language in this work. Each photon in (
1
) has two helicities, as a result there are 16 scalar amplitudes which fully describe the process (
1
). For simplicity we assume parity invariance in this work.
3
3

3

Due to CPT symmetry in the case of neutral identical particles (as in this paper) parity invariance implies time-reversal invariance.

Taking into account the fact that the particles under consideration are also identical we are left with only 5 different amplitudes:

++\to++,\qquad++\to--,\qquad+-\to+-,\qquad+-\to-+,\qquad++\to+-,

(2)

where
\pm
correspond to the helicities of the particles in (
1
).
We denote the corresponding amplitudes in the center of mass frame by

\Phi_{1}(s,t,u),\qquad\Phi_{2}(s,t,u),\qquad\Phi_{3}(s,t,u),\qquad\Phi_{4}(s,t,u),\qquad\Phi_{5}(s,t,u),

(3)

where
s
,
t
and
u
are the Mandelstam variables describing the scattering process and obeying the standard relation

s+t+u=0.

(4)

Due to crossing equations only the
\Phi_{1}
,
\Phi_{2}
, and
\Phi_{5}
amplitudes are independent, the rest can be related to these as

\Phi_{3}(s,t,u)=\Phi_{1}(u,t,s),\qquad\Phi_{4}(s,t,u)=\Phi_{1}(t,s,u).

(5)

Moreover, crossing also implies that
\Phi_{1}(s,t,u)
is symmetric under
t-u
permutation and the amplitudes
\Phi_{2}(s,t,u)
and
\Phi_{5}(s,t,u)
are fully symmetric under permutations of their arguments.
We give the precise definition of the amplitudes (
3
) in appendix
A
. There we also derive their crossing equations and unitarity constraints. For completeness we also explain how to define the amplitudes via tensor structures in appendix
B
both in vector and spinor formalisms.

Non-perturbative observables

At low energy, the amplitudes
\Phi_{1}
,
\Phi_{2}
and
\Phi_{5}
have the following expansion:
4
4

4

Let us emphasize that even though we write
\mathcal{O}(s^{n})
, the expansion is at small
s\sim t\sim u
and
n
is the total power of
s,t,u
.

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=g_{2}s^{2}+g_{3}s^{3}+g_{4}s^{4}+g_{4}^{\prime}s^{2}tu+L_{1}(s|t,u)+\mathcal{O}(s^{5}),

(6)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=f_{2}(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2})+f_{3}stu+f_{4}(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2})^{2}

\displaystyle+L_{2}(s,t,u)+\mathcal{O}(s^{5}),

\displaystyle\Phi_{5}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=h_{3}\,stu+\mathcal{O}(s^{5})~.

The two functions
L_{1}(s,t,u)
and
L_{2}(s,t,u)
are fixed by unitarity in terms of the polynomial terms in (
6
), see appendix
C
:

\displaystyle L_{1}(s|t,u)

\displaystyle\equiv s^{2}\left(\beta_{1,1}s^{2}+\beta_{1,2}tu\right)\log\left(-s\sqrt{g_{2}}\right)+\beta_{1,3}s^{2}\left(t^{2}\log\left(-t\sqrt{g_{2}}\right)+u^{2}\log\left(-u\sqrt{g_{2}}\right)\right),

\displaystyle L_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle\equiv\beta_{2}\left(s^{4}\log\left(-s\sqrt{g_{2}}\right)+t^{4}\log\left(-t\sqrt{g_{2}}\right)+u^{4}\log\left(-u\sqrt{g_{2}}\right)\right),

(7)

where the coefficients
\beta
read as

\displaystyle\beta_{1,1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{14f_{2}^{2}+5g_{2}^{2}}{160\pi^{2}},\qquad

\displaystyle\beta_{1,2}=\frac{f_{2}^{2}}{240\pi^{2}}

(8)

\displaystyle\beta_{1,3}

\displaystyle=-\frac{g_{2}^{2}}{80\pi^{2}},\qquad

\displaystyle\beta_{2}=-\frac{5f_{2}g_{2}}{48\pi^{2}}.

Equations (
6
) - (
8
) follow from a few simple assumptions, which are compactly encoded by an effective field theory (EFT), to be discussed below.
5
5

5

The amplitudes (
6
) can also be derived from softness, unitarity, crossing symmetry and kinematical contraints.
Softness is the assumption that the 2-to-2 amplitude scales like
(\textup{energy})^{4}
, and that the 2-to-
(n\geq 3)
amplitude scales at least like
(\textup{energy})^{6}
at low energy.
Then unitarity fixes the coefficient of the
\log
terms as in (
8
), see appendix
C
.
Crossing symmetry restricts the polynomials of
s
,
t
and
u
that can appear in (
6
). Finally, the kinematical contraints discussed in
Hebbar:2020ukp
(see eq. (2.139)), imply that
\Phi_{3}=u^{2}(...)
and
\Phi_{5}=stu(...)
.

For now, it suffices to know that the main ingredient is the absence of other massless degrees of freedom beyond the spin one particle
\gamma
.

We refer to the real parameters
g_{n}
,
f_{n}
and
h_{n}
in (
16
) as non-perturbative observables. Our notation is almost identical to the one of
Henriksson:2021ymi
;
Henriksson:2022oeu
6
6

6

The difference between our observables (in black) and the ones of
Henriksson:2021ymi
;
Henriksson:2022oeu
(in blue) is the coefficients
g_{4}
and
g_{4}^{\prime}
which are related to their
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}g_{4,1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}g_{4,2}}
as follows

\displaystyle g_{4}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}g_{4,1}+2g_{4,2}},\qquad g_{4}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-2g_{4,2}}.

apart from the fact that we take into account the logarithmic branch cuts associated to intermediate massless particles.
As it will be shown, the parameter
g_{2}
is always non-negative, thus the object
\sqrt{g_{2}}
entering inside the log terms is unambiguous.

For complete clarity, let us emphasize that the observables
g_{n}
,
f_{n}
and
h_{n}
are well defined and measurable in terms of derivatives of the non-perturbative scattering amplitude. At the
n\leq 3
level we have

g_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{s}^{2}\Phi_{1}(0,0,0),\qquad g_{3}=\frac{1}{3!}\partial_{s}^{3}\Phi_{1}(0,0,0),

(9)

together with

2f_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{s}^{2}\Phi_{2}(0,0,0),\qquad-f_{3}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{s}^{2}\partial_{t}\Phi_{2}(0,0,0),\qquad-h_{3}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{s}^{2}\partial_{t}\Phi_{5}(0,0,0).

(10)

At the
n=4
level we have instead

\displaystyle g_{4}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{4!}\partial_{s}^{4}\left(\Phi_{1}(s,t,-s-t)-L_{1}(s|t,-s-t)\right),

(11)

\displaystyle-g^{\prime}_{4}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{3!}\partial_{s}^{3}\partial_{t}\left(\Phi_{1}(s,t,-s-t)-L_{1}(s|t,-s-t)\right),

\displaystyle 4f_{4}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{4!}\partial_{s}^{4}\left(\Phi_{2}(s,t,-s-t)-L_{2}(s,t,-s-t)\right).

We choose
g_{2}
to define an energy scale. It is then convenient to define the following dimensionless observables

\bar{f}_{2}\equiv\frac{f_{2}}{g_{2}},\quad\bar{f}_{3}\equiv\frac{f_{3}}{g_{2}^{3/2}},\quad\bar{g}_{3}\equiv\frac{g_{3}}{g_{2}^{3/2}},\quad\bar{h}_{3}\equiv\frac{h_{3}}{g_{2}^{3/2}},\quad\overline{f}_{4}\equiv\frac{f_{4}}{g_{2}^{2}},\quad\overline{g}_{4}\equiv\frac{g_{4}}{g_{2}^{2}},\quad\overline{g}_{4}^{\prime}\equiv\frac{g_{4}^{\prime}}{g_{2}^{2}}.

(12)

Effective field theory

From the QFT perspective there is only one consistent way to describe a massless spin one particle, namely as a
U(1)
gauge theory. We use this fact to construct an effective field theory (EFT) Lagrangian density that can be used to describe the process (
1
) at low energies. This is given by summing all possible linearly independent Lorentz invariants built out of the electromagnetic tensor
F_{\mu\nu}
with some generic coefficients. The most general form of such a Lagrangian density reads as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{EFT}}=-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}+\mathcal{L}_{6}+\mathcal{L}_{8}+\mathcal{L}_{10}+\mathcal{L}_{12}+\ldots,

(13)

where
\mathcal{L}_{n}
denotes terms with mass dimension
n
. Explicitly they read as
7
7

7

We do not include terms with more than four factors of
F_{\mu\nu}
because these do not contribute to the process (
1
) to the order in
s,\,t,\,u
we are considering.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{6}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{8}

\displaystyle=c_{1}(F_{\mu\nu}F^{\nu\mu})(F_{\alpha\beta}F^{\beta\alpha})+c_{2}(F_{\mu\nu}F^{\nu\rho}F_{\rho\sigma}F^{\sigma\mu}),

(14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{10}

\displaystyle=c_{3}(F_{\alpha\beta}\partial^{\beta}F_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\alpha}F^{\nu\rho}{F_{\rho}}^{\mu})+c_{4}(\partial_{\alpha}F_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\alpha}F^{\nu\mu})(F_{\rho\sigma}F^{\sigma\rho})+c_{5}(\partial_{\alpha}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\nu\rho}\partial^{\alpha}F_{\rho\sigma}F^{\sigma\mu}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{12}

\displaystyle=c_{6}(\partial_{\nu}F_{\alpha\rho}\partial^{\alpha}F^{\nu\sigma}\partial^{\delta}F^{\beta\rho}\partial_{\beta}F_{\delta\sigma})+c_{7}(F_{\rho\beta}\partial_{\alpha}F_{\sigma\gamma}\partial^{\beta}F^{\sigma\delta}\partial_{\delta}\partial^{\gamma}F^{\rho\alpha})+c_{8}(F_{\alpha\beta}F_{\sigma\gamma}\delta^{\alpha}\delta_{\rho}F^{\gamma\delta}\partial^{\beta}\partial_{\delta}F^{\rho\sigma}).

The EFT description is valid up to some cut-off scale which we denote by
M
.
The real dimensionful coefficients
c_{i}
are called Wilson coefficients. They have the following mass dimensions

[c_{1}]=[c_{2}]=-4,\qquad[c_{3}]=[c_{4}]=[c_{5}]=-6,\qquad[c_{6}]=[c_{7}]=[c_{8}]=-8.

(15)

Some recent experimental bounds on some of these Wilson coefficients can be found in
Aaboud:2017bwk
;
Ellis:2022uxv
.

Using the EFT Lagrangian density we can compute scattering amplitudes of massless particles
\Phi_{i}(s,t,u)
. We denote them by
\Phi^{\text{EFT}}_{i}(s,t,u)
. The details of this computation, to 1-loop order, are provided in appendix
D
. The amplitudes
\Phi^{\text{EFT}}_{i}(s,t,u)
are good approximations of the full non-perturbative amplitudes
\Phi_{i}(s,t,u)
in the regime
|s|\lesssim M^{2}
, namely

i=1,2,5:\qquad\Phi_{i}(s,t,u)\approx\Phi^{\text{EFT}}_{i}(s,t,u),\qquad|s|\lesssim M^{2}.

(16)

This is depicted in figure
1
.
The amplitudes
\Phi^{\text{EFT}}_{i}(s,t,u)
will have precisely the same form as in (
6
) given the relations

\displaystyle g_{2}=2(4c_{1}+3c_{2}),\qquad f_{2}=2(4c_{1}+c_{2}),

(17)

\displaystyle g_{3}=4c_{4},\qquad f_{3}=6(c_{3}+2c_{4}-c_{5}),\qquad h_{3}=\frac{3}{2}c_{3},

\displaystyle g_{4}=\frac{1}{4}(-3c_{6}+2c_{7}),\qquad g_{4}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}(3c_{6}-2c_{7}-c_{8}),\qquad f_{4}=-\frac{c_{6}}{8}.

In the computation above we used dimensional regularization and we have chosen our renormalization scale
\mu
to be
\mu^{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{g_{2}}}
.

s
\bullet
0
\bullet
-t
M^{2}

Figure 1:
Analytic structure of the full non-perturbative amplitudes
\Phi_{i}(s,t,u)
in the complex plane
s
at a fixed value of
t<0
. There are two branch cuts along the real axis, namely, for
s>0
and for
u=-s-t>0
. The EFT amplitudes are good approximations of the full non-perturbative amplitude inside the grey region corresponding to
|s|\lesssim M^{2}
.
The full non-perturbative amplitude may have other branch points on the real axis. For example, in QED there are branch points at
s=4m_{e}^{2}
and
u=4m_{e}^{2}
associated with the threshold for electron-positron production.

Partial amplitudes and unitarity

Given the scattering amplitudes (
3
) one defines partial amplitudes as follows

{\Phi_{i}^{\ell}}(s)\equiv\frac{1}{32\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\sin\theta\,d^{\,\ell}_{\lambda_{12},\lambda_{34}}(\theta)\;\Phi_{i}\left(s,t(s,\theta),u(s,\theta)\right)

(18)

where the scattering angle
\theta
is related to the Mandelstam variables as

t=-\frac{s}{2}(1-\cos\theta),\quad u=-\frac{s}{2}(1+\cos\theta).

(19)

The small Wigner
d
-matrix is defined in (
119
). The differences of helicities are defined as

\lambda_{12}\equiv\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2},\qquad\lambda_{34}\equiv\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}.

(20)

The values of helicities for the amplitudes
\Phi_{i}(s,t,u)
are given in (
2
).

Using partial amplitudes we can write full non-linear unitarity constraints in simple positive semi-definite form as

\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{I}&\mathbb{S}^{\dagger}(s)\\
\mathbb{S}(s)&\mathbb{I}\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0,

(21)

where
\mathbb{I}
is the (appropriate) identity matrix and

\mathbb{S}(s)=\begin{cases}1+i(\Phi_{1}^{0}(s)+\Phi_{2}^{0}(s)),\qquad\ell=0,\\
1+i(\Phi_{1}^{\ell}(s)-\Phi_{2}^{\ell}(s)),\qquad\ell\geq 0\text{ (even)},\\
1+2i\Phi_{3}^{\ell}(s),\qquad\qquad\quad\;\;\;\ell\geq 3\text{ (odd)},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}+i\begin{pmatrix}\Phi_{1}^{\ell}(s)+\Phi_{2}^{\ell}(s)&\qquad 2\Phi_{5}^{\ell}(s)\\
2\Phi_{5}^{\ell}(s)&\qquad 2\Phi_{3}^{\ell}(s)\end{pmatrix},\qquad\ell\geq 2\text{ (even)}.\end{cases}

(22)

These conditions hold in the physical regime
s\geq 0
.

There is a simpler subset of the above constraints (called positivity) given by

\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{F}(s)\succeq 0,

(23)

where
\mathbb{F}
is defined via
\mathbb{S}
as follows

\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{I}+i\mathbb{F}\,.

(24)

A subset (the linear part) of these positivity constraints read as

\begin{split}&\ell\geq 0\text{ (even)}:\qquad\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{1}^{\ell}(s)+\Phi^{\ell}_{2}(s))\geq 0,\\
&\ell\geq 0\text{ (even)}:\qquad\operatorname{Im}(\Phi^{\ell}_{1}(s)-\Phi^{\ell}_{2}(s))\geq 0,\\
&\ell\geq 2:\qquad\qquad\quad\operatorname{Im}\Phi^{\ell}_{3}(s)\geq 0.\end{split}

(25)

Goal of the paper

In this paper we study bounds on the dimensionless observables (
12
) coming from full non-linear unitarity (
21
).

Bounds from positivity

Using dispersion relations it is relatively easy to incorporate the positivity constraints (
25
) and obtain analytic bounds on the observables. This is addressed in section
2
. Here we briefly summarize the results from that section.

For the observables
g_{2}
and
f_{2}
we obtain the following rigorous bounds

-1\leq\frac{f_{2}}{g_{2}}\leq 1,\qquad g_{2}\geq 0.

(26)

We could not derive any bound on
g_{3}
,
f_{3}
and
h_{3}
using dispersion relations and positivity.
There are simple dispersion relations for
g_{4}
and
f_{4}
, however due to the presence of log terms in (
6
) we cannot derive bounds on these observables.
More precisely, we find

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{4}\pm 2\bar{f}_{4}

\displaystyle\geq 0+\underbrace{\frac{42\bar{f}_{2}^{2}\pm 50\bar{f}_{2}+21}{480\pi^{2}}}_{>0}\log(\hat{s}\sqrt{g_{2}})+\mathcal{O}(\hat{s}\sqrt{g_{2}})\,,

(27)

where
0<\hat{s}\lesssim M^{2}
.
The first (zero) term in the right-hand side of (
27
) was obtained in
Henriksson:2021ymi
;
Henriksson:2022oeu
neglecting the branch cuts from photon loops. The second term in these expressions is a novel result obtained by taking into account the
log
terms.
We would like to consider
\hat{s}\rightarrow 0
to drop the error term.
However, in this limit, the bound (
27
) is useless because the
log
terms diverge.

More bounds similar to these can be derived—see for example
Guerrieri:2020bto
;
Bellazzini:2020cot
;
Bellazzini:2021oaj
for bounds including IR
logs
. For bounds on EFTs from positivity derived in various other contexts, see
Caron-Huot:2020cmc
;
Caron-Huot:2021rmr
;
Davighi:2021osh
;
deRham:2021fpu
;
Henriksson:2021ymi
;
Henriksson:2022oeu
;
Caron-Huot:2022ugt
;
Tolley:2020gtv
;
deRham:2021bll
;
deRham:2022hpx
;
Chiang:2022ltp
;
Caron-Huot:2022jli
.

Bounds from full non-linear unitarity

We use the primal numerical approach of
Paulos:2017fhb
;
Homrich:2019cbt
to bound the observables (
12
) using full non-linear unitarity (
21
). This is done in section
3
. We briefly summarize our results here.

First, we found numerically that the bound on
\bar{f}_{2}
is identical to (
26
) which was found by using positivity only. Second, we found that neither upper nor lower bounds exist on the obervables
\bar{g}_{3}
,
\bar{f}_{3}
and
\bar{h}_{3}
. Finally, we discovered that there exists a lower bound on
\bar{g}_{4}
. The bound is presented in figures
2
-
4
. There the lower bound on
\bar{g}_{4}
is constructed as a function of
\bar{f}_{2}
,
\bar{f}_{4}
and
\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}
respectively.

All our numerical data can be downloaded from
https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.7308006
.

The absence of bounds

As stated in the previous paragraph, we often see that there is no bound on a given observable. We explain this fact in section
4
by explicitly constructing weakly coupled theories which satisfy all our assumptions and have unbounded observables. For instance we show analytically that no bounds exist on
\bar{g}_{3}
,
\bar{f}_{3}
and
\bar{h}_{3}
.

Figure 2:
Lower bound on the observable
\bar{g}_{4}
as a function of
\bar{f}_{2}
. The allowed region is shaded in light blue. The estimated error is indicated by dark blue.

Figure 3:
Lower bound on the
\bar{g}_{4}
observable as a function
\bar{f}_{4}
for a given value of
\bar{f}_{2}
. The allowed region lies above the colored lines. The dashed line described by
\bar{g}_{4}=2|\bar{f}_{4}|
is placed for reference.

Figure 4:
Lower bound on the
\bar{g}_{4}
observable as a function
\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}
for a given value of
\bar{f}_{2}
. The allowed region lies above the colored lines.

2
Bounds from positivity and constraints on EFTs

In this section we will write relations which allow to express the observables (
12
) or (
92
) as integrals of the amplitudes
\Phi_{i}(s,t,u)
. These relations are called dispersion relations. For simplicity we will work in the forward limit
t=0
. Combining dispersion relations with positivity (
25
) allows us to bound our observables. In what follows we will focus only on
g_{2}
,
f_{2}
,
g_{4}
and
f_{4}
.

Positivity constraints

Positivity constraints were given in (
25
) in terms of partial amplitudes. These constraints can be equivalently translated into amplitudes in the forward limit using the inverse of (
18
). We get then

\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{1}(s)\pm\Phi_{2}(s))\geq 0,\qquad\operatorname{Im}\Phi_{3}(s)\geq 0.

(28)

We independently derive these constraints also in appendix
A.4
.
Here and in the rest of this section we use the following short-hand notation for the forward amplitudes

\Phi_{i}(s)\equiv\Phi_{i}(s,\,t=0,\,u=-s).

(29)

Dispersion relations

Let us start by defining the following functions

V_{n}^{\pm}(s)\equiv\frac{\Phi_{1}(s)\pm\Phi_{2}(s)+\Phi_{3}(s)}{s^{n+1}}

(30)

in the
s
complex plane.
The functions
V_{n}^{\pm}(s)
have an analytic structure inherited from the functions
\Phi_{i}(s,t,u)
as depicted in figure
1
and an additional pole at
s=0
. The functions
V_{n}^{\pm}(s)
obey
V_{n}^{\pm}(-s)=-V_{n}^{\pm}(s)
for even
n
due to
s-u
crossing symmetry in equation (
5
) and the discussion below it. Finally, the imaginary part of the functions
V_{n}^{\pm}(s)
is non-negative due to positivity constraints (
28
) for
s>0
.

Integrating
V_{n}^{\pm}(s)
over a closed contour as depicted in figure
5
we get

\oint dsV_{n}^{\pm}(s)=0

(31)

since
V_{n}^{\pm}(s)
are analytic inside this contour of integration.
The contour has several pieces: the small arc with radius
\hat{s}
denoted by
\gamma
, the big arc with infinitely large radius denoted by
\Gamma
and two horizontal stretches.
Splitting the integral in (
31
) into these pieces we get

\int_{\infty}^{\hat{s}}dsV_{n}^{\pm}(s)+\int_{-\hat{s}}^{-\infty}dsV_{n}^{\pm}(s)+\int_{\gamma}dsV_{n}^{\pm}(s)+\int_{\Gamma}dsV_{n}^{\pm}(s)=0.

(32)

Assuming the analogue of the Martin-Froissart bound for spin one massless particles
8
8

8

For a recent discussion of the Froissart bound in the case of massless spin two particles see
Haring:2022cyf
.
we get

\lim_{|s|\rightarrow\infty}s\,V_{n}^{\pm}(s)=0\,,\quad\text{for }n\geq 2

(33)

As a result the integral over the large arc
\Gamma
vanishes

\int_{\Gamma}dsV_{n}^{\pm}(s)=0.

(34)

Using
s-u
crossing symmetry we get
9
9

9

The first integral is evaluated slightly above the right branch cute. By crossing symmetry, the left integral is related to the integral slightly below the right branch cut. The sum of the two terms gives a discontinuity which in turn is related to the imaginary part of the amplitude.

-\left(\int_{\infty}^{\hat{s}}dsV_{n}^{\pm}(s)+\int_{-\hat{s}}^{-\infty}dsV_{n}^{\pm}(s)\right)=2i\,\int_{\hat{s}}^{\infty}ds\operatorname{Im}V_{n}^{\pm}(s).

(35)

In order to evaluate the third integral in (
32
) we use the representation (
6
) of the amplitudes which is valid for
|s|\lesssim M^{2}
. Performing the change of variables
s=\hat{s}\cos\phi
, where
\phi\in[0;\pi]
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\gamma}dsV_{2}^{\pm}(s)

\displaystyle=\pi i\,2(g_{2}\pm f_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\hat{s}^{2}/M^{8}),

(36)

\displaystyle\int_{\gamma}dsV_{4}^{\pm}(s)

\displaystyle=\pi i\,2\left(g_{4}\pm 2f_{4}+\left(\beta_{1,1}+\beta_{1,3}\ \pm\beta_{2}\right)\log\left(\hat{s}\sqrt{g_{2}}\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}(\hat{s}/M^{10})\,,

where we estimated the error using the expected EFT scaling of Wilson coefficients with the cutoff scale
M
.

Plugging equations (
34
) - (
36
) into (
32
) we finally obtain the following dispersion relations

g_{2}\pm f_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\hat{s}^{2}/M^{8})=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\hat{s}}^{\infty}ds\frac{\operatorname{Im}[\Phi_{1}(s)\pm\Phi_{2}(s)+\Phi_{3}(s)]}{s^{3}},

(37)

together with

g_{4}\pm 2f_{4}+\left(\beta_{1,1}+\beta_{1,3}\ \pm\beta_{2}\right)\log\left(\hat{s}\sqrt{g_{2}}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\hat{s}/M^{10})=\\
\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\hat{s}}^{\infty}ds\frac{\operatorname{Im}[\Phi_{1}(s)\pm\Phi_{2}(s)+\Phi_{3}(s)]}{s^{5}}.

(38)

\gamma
\Gamma
s
\hat{s}
\bullet

Figure 5:
Integration contour of the analytic functions
V_{n}^{\pm}
defined in (
30
) in the
s
complex plane. The functions
V_{n}^{\pm}
have the same analytic structures as in figure
1
. Compared to figure
1
they have however an additional pole at
s=0
indicated by a green dot.

Bounds on
g_{2}
and
f_{2}

Consider the dispersion relation (
37
) and
take the limit
\hat{s}\rightarrow 0
. In this limit the combination
g_{2}\pm f_{2}
is related to a certain integral of the amplitudes. Due to positivity in the form (
28
) this integral is non-negative and we obtain the following rigorous inequalities

g_{2}\geq 0,\qquad-1\leq\frac{f_{2}}{g_{2}}\leq 1.

(39)

Bounds on
g_{4}
and
f_{4}

Consider now the dispersion relation (
38
). Naively it is impossible to take the limit
\hat{s}\rightarrow 0
because there is a log term in the left-hand side which diverges in this limit. Nevertheless, using positivity conditions (
28
) we arrive at

g_{4}\pm 2f_{4}+\underbrace{\left(\beta_{1,1}+\beta_{1,3}\ \pm\beta_{2}\right)}_{<0}\log\left(\hat{s}\sqrt{g_{2}}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\hat{s}/M^{10})\geq 0.

(40)

This relation holds for any
\hat{s}
in the range
\hat{s}\lesssim M^{2}
.
Unfortunately this relation is not very useful because either the error term is large (for
\hat{s}
large but still below the cutoff scale
M^{2}
) or the
\log
term is large (for
\hat{s}
small).
Dividing by
g_{2}^{2}
, we obtain a dimensionless version of (
40
):

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{4}\pm 2\bar{f}_{4}

\displaystyle\geq 0+\underbrace{\frac{42\bar{f}_{2}^{2}\pm 50\bar{f}_{2}+21}{480\pi^{2}}}_{>0}\log(\hat{s}\sqrt{g_{2}})+\mathcal{O}(\hat{s}\sqrt{g_{2}})\,,

(41)

which we quoted in the introduction. Here we further assumed that
g_{2}\sim M^{-4}
to simplify the error term.

It is actually possible to take the limit
\hat{s}\rightarrow 0
inside (
38
). To this end, consider the following representation of the logarithm

\log(x)=\int_{x}^{\infty}dy\frac{-1}{y(1+y)}+\mathcal{O}(x).

(42)

It allows to bring the result (
38
) into the following form

g_{4}\pm 2f_{4}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\hat{s}}^{\infty}ds\left(\frac{\operatorname{Im}[\Phi_{1}(s)\pm\Phi_{2}(s)+\Phi_{3}(s)]}{s^{5}}+\frac{\pi(\beta_{1,1}+\beta_{1,3}\pm\beta_{2})}{s(1+s\sqrt{g_{2}})}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\hat{s}/M^{10}).

(43)

The integrand here is well-defined at
\hat{s}\rightarrow 0
since the divergence of the first term governed by (
6
) cancels the divergence of the second term. We can thus write an explicit integral form of
g_{4}\pm 2f_{4}
as

g_{4}\pm 2f_{4}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\left(\frac{\operatorname{Im}[\Phi_{1}(s)\pm\Phi_{2}(s)+\Phi_{3}(s)]}{s^{5}}+\frac{\pi(\beta_{1,1}+\beta_{1,3}\pm\beta_{2})}{s(1+s\sqrt{g_{2}})}\right).

(44)

Let us now notice that

\beta_{1,1}+\beta_{1,3}\pm\beta_{2}<0.

(45)

This means that the second term in the integrand in (
44
) is negative whereas the first term in the integrand is non-negative due to positivity. As a result the integrand in (
44
) does not have definite sign and no positivity bound can be deduced on the simple combination
g_{4}\pm 2f_{4}
.

3
Bounds from full non-linear unitarity

In this section we present our numerical bounds. We start in subsection
3.1
by explaining our numerical setup. We will then use it to bound the coefficient
\bar{f}_{2}
in subsection
3.2
. We will explore bounds on
\bar{g}_{3}
,
\bar{f}_{3}
and
\bar{h}_{3}
in subsection
3.3
. We will study bounds on
\bar{g}_{4}
,
\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}
and
\bar{f}_{4}
in subsection
3.4
. In subsection
3.5
we show in all generality that parameters appearing linearly in a scattering amplitude are subject at most to one-sided bounds in the non-perturbative S-matrix bootstrap.
Finally, in subsection
3.6
we test the low spin dominance conjecture using our data.

We make our numerical data public. It can be downloaded from

https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.7308006
.

3.1
Numerical setup

We consider the following non-perturbative ansatz for the three independent amplitudes
10
10

10

The prefactors in front of
\Phi_{1}\,,\Phi_{5}
use the variable
\chi
defined below (
48
). They are included so that at low energy, the prefactors become those of the spinor-helicity amplitudes (
265
).

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\chi_{s}^{2}\sum_{a,b,c=0}^{N_{\text{max}}}\alpha^{1}_{abc}\rho_{s}^{a}\rho_{t}^{b}\rho_{u}^{c}+\mathbb{L}_{1}(s|t,u),

(46)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,c=0}^{N_{\text{max}}}\alpha^{2}_{abc}\rho_{s}^{a}\rho_{t}^{b}\rho_{u}^{c}+\mathbb{L}_{2}(s,t,u),

\displaystyle\Phi_{5}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=(-\chi_{s}\chi_{t}\chi_{u})\sum_{a,b,c=0}^{N_{\text{max}}}\alpha^{5}_{abc}\rho_{s}^{a}\rho_{t}^{b}\rho_{u}^{c}.

Here
\alpha^{i}_{abc}
are real dimensionless parameters. Due to crossing symmetry
\alpha^{1}_{abc}=\alpha^{1}_{acb}
and
\alpha^{2}_{abc}
,
\alpha^{5}_{abc}
are fully symmetric in their indices. The
\rho
-variable is defined as

\rho_{s}\equiv\frac{\sqrt{-s_{0}}-\sqrt{-s}}{\sqrt{-s_{0}}+\sqrt{-s}}.

(47)

where
s_{0}<0
is a free real parameter. The
\chi
-variable is defined as

\chi_{s}\equiv\frac{1}{4}(\rho_{s}-1)^{2}-\frac{1}{4}(\rho_{s}-1)^{3}=\frac{s}{s_{0}}-3\left(\frac{s}{s_{0}}\right)^{2}+\mathcal{O}(s^{5/2}).

(48)

This variable was introduced in
Guerrieri:2020bto
, and it is built in such a way that it becomes a constant at high energy.
It is also convenient to define the following object

P\equiv\frac{1}{8}(1+\rho_{s})(1+\rho_{t})(1+\rho_{u}).

(49)

At fixed scattering angle
\theta
(defined using the Mandelstam variables in (
105
)) and small value of
s
it behaves as
1
and at fixed angle and large value of
s
it decays as
\mathcal{O}(s^{-3/2})
.
Finally the functions
\mathbb{L}_{1}
and
\mathbb{L}_{2}
are defined as
11
11

11

The prefactor
P
is introduced here in order to make the functions
\mathbb{L}_{1}(s|t,u)
and
\mathbb{L}_{2}(s,t,u)
decay fast enough at large values of
s
. This allows to use the large energy constraints derived in appendix
H.2
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{1}(s|t,u)

\displaystyle\equiv s_{0}^{4}\,P\,\chi_{s}^{2}\left[\beta_{1,1}\chi_{s}^{2}\log\chi_{s}+\beta_{1,2}\chi_{t}\chi_{u}\log\chi_{s}+\beta_{1,3}(\chi_{t}^{2}\log\chi_{t}+\chi_{u}^{2}\log\chi_{u})\right],

(50)

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle\equiv s_{0}^{4}\,P\,\beta_{2}(\chi_{s}^{4}\log\chi_{s}+\chi_{t}^{4}\log\chi_{t}+\chi_{u}^{4}\log\chi_{u}).

These functions have the following low energy expansion:

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{1}(s|t,u)

\displaystyle=s^{2}\left[\beta_{1,1}s^{2}\log(s/s_{0})+\beta_{1,2}tu\log(s/s_{0})+\beta_{1,3}(t^{2}\log(t/s_{0})+u^{2}\log(u/s_{0}))\right]+\mathcal{O}(s^{5}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\beta_{2}(s^{4}\log(s/s_{0})+t^{4}\log(t/s_{0})+u^{4}\log(u/s_{0}))+\mathcal{O}(s^{5}).

Comparing them with (
7
) we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{1}(s|t,u)

\displaystyle=L_{1}(s|t,u)-s^{2}\left[\beta_{1,1}s^{2}+\beta_{1,2}tu+\beta_{1,3}(t^{2}+u^{2})\right]\log(-s_{0}\sqrt{g_{2}})+\mathcal{O}(s^{5}),

(51a)

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=L_{2}(s,t,u)-\beta_{2}(s^{4}+t^{4}+u^{4})\log(-s_{0}\sqrt{g_{2}})+\mathcal{O}(s^{5}).

(51b)

As was discussed in the introduction, scattering amplitudes of any massless spin one particles in the vicinity of
s=0
have the representation (
6
). We, thus, need to expand (
46
) around
s=0
taking into account (
51
) and match the result with (
6
). This procedure will generate a set of linear constraints on the parameters of the ansatz
\alpha
. Solving these constraints and plugging the solution back into (
46
) we obtain the final form of the ansatz which depends on the following parameters

\{s_{0};g_{2},g_{3},g_{4},g_{4}^{\prime},f_{2},f_{3},f_{4},h_{3},\,\text{rest of }\alpha s\}.

(52)

All the coefficients in this list enter the ansatz linearly except for
s_{0}
,
g_{2}
and
f_{2}
. Squares of
g_{2}
and
f_{2}
multiply the log terms. The former also enters inside the log terms.

The ansatz (
46
) has a finite number of terms controlled by the parameter
N_{\text{max}}
. All the numerical results depend on this parameter. The true bound is obtained by extrapolating the numerical results to
N_{\text{max}}=\infty
.

In order to impose non-linear unitarity we first compute partial amplitudes by plugging (
46
) into the definition (
18
). The integrals are evaluated numerically in Mathematica. They will depend on the set of parameters (
52
). Plugging them into (
21
) we obtain a set of unitarity constraints. We impose these constraints for a finite number of spins
\ell=0,\ldots,L_{\text{max}}
. All the numerical results also depend on the parameter
L_{\text{max}}
and thus require an extrapolation to
L_{\text{max}}=\infty
. We carefully discuss the extrapolation procedure for both
N_{\text{max}}
and
L_{\text{max}}
in subsection
3.4.1
. In order to improve the convergence with
L_{\text{max}}
, we add the unitarity constraints in the limit
L_{\text{max}}=\infty
. This is discussed in appendix
H.1
. The convergence is also improved by adding the amplitude positivity constraint in the forward limit
(
153
). As
L_{\text{max}}\to\infty
, these constraints are included in the full unitarity constraints imposed numerically (
21
) via the positivity (
25
). However, for any finite
L_{\text{max}}
, (
153
) contains more information.

The unitarity constraints (
21
) should be imposed for all
s\geq 0
. In practice we pick a finite grid of
s
values where we impose unitarity. The points are chosen using the Chebyshev distribution in the
\rho_{s}
variable (see
e.g.
footnote 34 in
Hebbar:2020ukp
). The number of points in this grid is denoted by
N_{\text{grid}}
. In all the calculations we use
N_{\text{grid}}=200
for unitarity constraints with spins
\ell\leq 50
and
N_{\text{grid}}=50
for unitarity constraints with spins
\ell>50
. We also include analytic constraints at
s=\infty
, for details see appendix
H.2
.
On top of the unitarity constraints (
21
) we also impose the positivity constraints (
28
) at
N_{\text{grid}}=200
values of
s
.

The amplitudes in
d=4
are dimensionless. We can thus rewrite the ansatz (
46
) in terms of dimensionless quantities only. This is done by using
g_{2}
as a scale and defining

\bar{s}\equiv\sqrt{g_{2}}\,s,\qquad\bar{t}\equiv\sqrt{g_{2}}\,t,\qquad\bar{u}\equiv\sqrt{g_{2}}\,u.

(53)

Identical definitions are understood to hold for
s_{0}
.
With these definitions the explicit dependence on
g_{2}
completely disappears in (
46
) and the parameters we are left with are precisely the ones given in (
12
). In other words the ansatz depends only on the following coefficients

\{\bar{s}_{0};\bar{g}_{3},\bar{g}_{4},\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime},\bar{f}_{2},\bar{f}_{3},\bar{f}_{4},\bar{h}_{3},\,\text{rest of }\alpha s\}

(54)

and
g_{2}
is not a free variable.
We can chose the parameter
\bar{s}_{0}
at our will. A particular choice does not play any role for significantly large values of
N_{\text{max}}
. It can happen however that for some values of these parameters the numerics converge better and it is desirable to search for their optimal values. In practice, this search is unfeasible since we do not want to numerically evaluate the partial amplitudes for different choices of
\bar{s}_{0}
.

There is however a trick which allows to keep a tunable parameter in the numerical setup. To achieve that, we keep the ansatz in the form where
g_{2}
enters explicitly. We then set

s_{0}=-1.

(55)

This sets the scale of the problem which makes all the other variables and parameters (like
s
or
g_{2}
) effectively dimensionless. The parameter
g_{2}
is now free and can be set to any value. The bounds on (
54
) do not depend on this choice in the limit
N_{\text{max}}=\infty
. However, for a particular value of
g_{2}
the numerics will converge faster. Faster convergence means in practice that at a fixed
N_{\text{max}}
there is some value of
g_{2}
which leads to a better result (
e.g.
if we look for a minimal value of some parameter, better result means lower minimum of this parameter). For each optimization problem, we always perform a scan in
g_{2}
first. This strategy was already used in
Guerrieri:2020bto
and
Guerrieri:2021ivu
.
12
12

12

We thank Andrea Guerrieri for emphasizing this idea.

The explicit details of this scan are explained below.
Scanning over
\bar{s}_{0}
and scanning over
g_{2}
given (
55
) is equivalent. This can be seen by using the definition (
53
) and (
55
) which lead to

\bar{s}_{0}=-\sqrt{g_{2}}.

(56)

We solve the following optimization problem: find the values of parameters (
54
) such that the unitarity conditions (
21
) are satisfied and one of the parameters in (
54
) has a minimal or maximal value. We construct optimization problems in Mathematica. For solving the optimization problems we use SDPB
Simmons-Duffin:2015qma
;
Landry:2019qug
. The summary of the parameters used in our numerics can be found in table
1
.

SDPB precision

832
(binary)

dualityGapThreshold

10^{-8}

integral precision

35
(decimal)

mathematica internal precision

200
(decimal)

N_{\text{grid}}

\left\{\begin{matrix}200&\ell\leq 50\\
50&\ell>50\end{matrix}\right.

Table 1:
Parameters in the numerical setup.

3.2
Bounds on
\bar{f}_{2}

The very first optimization problem we would like to address is:
what are the maximal and minimal allowed values of the observable
\bar{f}_{2}
. For solving this optimization problem, we set the
\beta
’s as free linear parameters of our ansatz (
50
).
13
13

13

One may think that including the constraints (
8
) can lead to stronger bounds on
\bar{f}_{2}
. However, the extremal amplitudes that give
\bar{f}_{2}\to\pm 1
are very weakly coupled and therefore the 1-loop
\log
terms are irrelevant.
The numerical solution of this optimization problem at a fixed value
N_{\text{max}}=20
and several values of
g_{2}
is presented in table
2
. Here we choose
L_{\text{max}}=50
. The numerics converges extremely fast for this run and no extrapolation to
N_{\text{max}}\rightarrow\infty
and
L_{\text{max}}\rightarrow\infty
is needed. From table
2
we conclude that

-1\lessapprox\bar{f}_{2}\lessapprox+1,

(57)

which is in perfect agreement with the positivity bound (
39
).
The solution is very stable and depends weakly on
g_{2}
. Nevertheless, we see that when
g_{2}\rightarrow 0
we get slightly better results (smaller minimum and greater maximum). This suggests that the extremal values of the bound
\bar{f}_{2}=\pm 1
are saturated by free theories, which have
g_{2}=0
.
14
14

14

Strictly speaking, the amplitude vanishes in free theory, and there is no meaning for the observables (
12
). What we mean by “free theories” in this section is, more precisely, the free limit of weakly coupled amplitudes, for which ratios of Wilson coefficients can have well defined limits.

Notice that the solution presented in table
2
is symmetric under the
\bar{f}_{2}\leftrightarrow-\bar{f}_{2}
exchange. This comes from the fact that the unitarity constraints (
21
) are symmetric under
\Phi_{2}\leftrightarrow-\Phi_{2}
when
\Phi_{5}=0
. Investigating the solution leading to table
2
we indeed see that
\Phi_{5}\approx 0
numerically. For further discussions of the symmetries of the unitarity constraints, see the last paragraph of appendix
A.3
.

The result obtained here can be seen as a non-trivial check of our numerical setup. Interestingly, using full unitarity we do not get stronger bounds on
\bar{f}_{2}
than equation (
39
), which follows from positivity only. This is easy to understand in light of the observation above: the bounds are saturated by the free limit of weakly coupled theories, for which the constraints coming from full non-linear unitarity are irrelevant. We review the free theories saturating this bound in subsection
4.3
.

g_{2}

\min(\bar{f}_{2})

\max(\bar{f}_{2})

10^{-2}

-0.998710

0.998710

1

-0.998619

0.998619

10^{2}

-0.991237

0.991237

Minimal and maximal allowed values of the
\bar{f}_{2}
observable for different choices of
g_{2}
. These results are obtained with
N_{\text{max}}=20
and
L_{\text{max}}=50
.

3.3
Bounding the
(\bar{g}_{3},\bar{f}_{3},\bar{h}_{3})
space

Let us now study the upper and lower bounds on the
\bar{g}_{3}
,
\bar{f}_{3}
and
\bar{h}_{3}
observables.

Recall that our ansatz has log terms encoded into the objects
\mathbb{L}_{1}(s|t,u)
and
\mathbb{L}_{2}(s,t,u)
defined in (
50
). The coefficients
\beta
in these expressions depend on
g_{2}
and
f_{2}
quadratically according to (
8
). Notice however that we can use our numerical procedure to determine only observables entering linearly in our ansatz. The terms with
g_{2}^{2}
are not a problem since we always fix the value of
g_{2}
. However, we need to take some care of terms containing
f_{2}^{2}
. One option is to simply set
\mathbb{L}_{1}(s|t,u)=0
and
\mathbb{L}_{2}(s,t,u)=0
in the ansatz since these terms only become important for bounding
\bar{g}_{4}
,
\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}
and
\bar{f}_{4}
. Another option is to also fix the value of
f_{2}
or equivalently the value of
\bar{f}_{2}
.
15
15

15

Yet another option is to keep
\beta
as free linear parameters of the ansatz (
50
).

We have realized both options in practice, in this section we present only the results of the second one. It is important to notice, though, that these options are inequivalent in principle. We will comment on this fact in subsection
3.5
.

Let us now look for the minimal and maximal values of the
\bar{g}_{3}
observable at some fixed value of
\bar{f}_{2}
. Concretely, we will use two values
\bar{f}_{2}=0
and
\bar{f}_{2}=3/10
. We work at
N_{\text{max}}=20
and
L_{\text{max}}=50
, and do not perform any extrapolation in these parameters. The solution of this optimization problem is presented in table
3
for different values of
g_{2}
. From this table we see that the optimal value of
g_{2}
is at
g_{2}=0
(when
g_{2}\rightarrow 0
we get the lowest minimum and the highest maximum). The results of table
3
suggest that the optimal solution is a free theory (because
g_{2}=0
) with

-\infty\leq\bar{g}_{3}\leq+\infty.

(58)

Exactly the same conclusion holds for
\bar{f}_{3}
and
\bar{h}_{3}
. We will confirm this finding by analytically constructing free theories with
g_{2}=0
,
\bar{g}_{3}=\pm\infty
,
\bar{f}_{3}=\pm\infty
and
\bar{h}_{3}=\pm\infty
in subsection
4.4
.

\bar{f}_{2}=0

\bar{f}_{2}=3/10

for different choices of

. These results are obtained with

and

.

3.4
Bounding the
(\bar{g}_{4},\bar{g}^{\prime}_{4},\bar{f}_{4})
space

Let us finally study bounds on the observables
\bar{g}_{4}
,
\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}
and
\bar{f}_{4}
. As explained in the previous subsection, all the bounds are obtained at some fixed values of
\bar{f}_{2}
. In subsection
3.4.1
we present our numerical results for the lower bound on
\bar{g}_{4}
as a function of
\bar{f}_{2}
. In subsection
3.4.2
we present our lower bound on
\bar{g}_{4}
as a function of
\bar{f}_{2}
and
\bar{f}_{4}
. Finally, in subsection
3.4.3
we present our lower bound on
\bar{g}_{4}
as a function of
\bar{f}_{2}
and
\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}
. No upper bound exists on
\bar{g}_{4}
and
\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}
, and from the results below one can conclude that neither upper nor lower bounds exist on
\bar{f}_{4}
, as in the case of the
\bar{g}_{3}
,
\bar{f}_{3}
and
\bar{h}_{3}
observables studied in the previous subsection. As for the lower bound on
\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}
, the numerics in that region converge poorly, so no conclusion can be drawn.
We will partly explain the presence of unbounded directions in subsection
4.5
, by explicitly constructing free theories with infinitely large values for the Wilson coefficients. In particular, we will also show that
\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}
cannot be bounded from below.

3.4.1
\min{\bar{g}_{4}}
vs.
\bar{f}_{2}

We start by minimizing
\bar{g}_{4}
at several fixed values of
\bar{f}_{2}
. The results of the numerical optimization are given in table
4
. This data is computed using
N_{\text{max}}=20
and
L_{\text{max}}=50
. It is clear from the table that the best convergence is achieved at
g_{2}\sim 100
, since this value of
g_{2}
gives the lowest minimum. In the reminder of subsection
3.4
we will always use
g_{2}=100
.

Table 4:
Lower bound on
\bar{g}_{4}
at different values of
g_{2}
and
\bar{f}_{2}
. The optimal lowest minimum is achieved at
g_{2}\sim 100
. The results are obtained with
N_{\text{max}}=20
and
L_{\text{max}}=50
.

In practice our bounds depend on the parameters
N_{\text{max}}
and
L_{\text{max}}
. The correct bound is obtained only in the limit
N_{\text{max}}\to\infty
and
L_{\text{max}}\to\infty
. Let us carefully discuss how one can estimate the correct lower bound on
\bar{g}_{4}
in this limit.

Let us begin by using the strategy employed in
Guerrieri:2021ivu
. In figure
7
we study the dependence of the
\bar{g}_{4}
bound on
L_{\text{max}}
at fixed values of
N_{\text{max}}
. For concreteness we take
\bar{f}_{2}=-3/11
which is the value in QED at leading order in the coupling. The numerical data is indicated by colored dots. In the left plot of figure
7
we see that the bound gets stronger when we increase
L_{\text{max}}
. For
N_{\text{max}}=20
the bound stabilizes around
L_{\text{max}}=30
and diverges around
L_{\text{max}}=100
. In the interval
L_{\text{max}}\in(30,100)
the bound is linear. We refer to this interval as the plateau. For larger values of
N_{\text{max}}
the divergence of the bounds begins at larger values of
L_{\text{max}}
. This can be explained as follows - for a fixed finite
N_{\text{max}}
, as we increase
L_{\text{max}}
, the number of unitarity constraints that the ansatz has to satisfy increases. At some point, the ansatz is not big enough to satisfy all of these constraints and therefore the bound that we get diverges. But if we then increase
N_{\text{max}}
, the ansatz is now bigger and therefore it can satisfy unitarity constraints for larger values of
L_{\text{max}}
. Nevertheless, even with this bigger
N_{\text{max}}
and hence a bigger ansatz, the bound will still diverge at some (larger)
L_{\text{max}}
and we must disregard the data after this point.

We therefore restrict our attention to the plateau
16
16

16

We find the plateau using the discrete derivative
\texttt{dDer}(1/L_{\text{max}})
. Then, we select points such that
\texttt{dDer}(1/L_{\text{max}})\leq X\cdot\min(\texttt{dDer})
, where
X
is a factor that can be chosen. In practice we chose
X=3
.

and use linear extrapolation in
1/L_{\text{max}}
to obtain the lower bound of
\bar{g}_{4}
at
L_{\text{max}}\rightarrow\infty
for fixed values of
N_{\text{max}}
. The extrapolations are indicated by solid lines in figure
7
. The right plot in figure
7
is a zoomed in version of the left plot. The circles around the numerical data there indicate the points which were included in the plateau.

Once we obtain the extrapolated bounds at
L_{\text{max}}\rightarrow\infty
we also do a linear extrapolation in
1/N_{\text{max}}
. The result is depicted in figure
7
in black. Black dots indicate extrapolated
L_{\text{max}}\rightarrow\infty
values. Black lines indicate linear extrapolation in
1/N_{\text{max}}
. From previous experience we expect the numerical data to be linear in
1/N_{\text{max}}
starting from
N_{\text{max}}\sim 20
. This is compatible with the results depicted in figure
7
.

Figure 6:
Lower bound on
\bar{g}_{4}
at fixed
\bar{f}_{2}=-3/11
(QED point). Colored dots indicate the numerical data. Solid lines indicate linear extrapolations in
1/L_{\text{max}}
. The right plot is a zoomed version of the left one. The circles around the numerical data indicate where the data is linear in
1/L_{\text{max}}
. The linear regions are called the plateaus. The linear extrapolation is performed only within each plateau.

Figure 7:
Lower bound on
\bar{g}_{4}
at fixed
\bar{f}_{2}=-3/11
(QED point). Colored dots denote the numerical data obtained at various fixed values of
L_{\text{max}}
. Black dots are obtained instead by performing
L_{\text{max}}\rightarrow\infty
extrapolation in figure
7
. Dashed lines indicate linear extrapolations in
1/N_{\text{max}}
to
N_{\text{max}}\rightarrow\infty
.

Figure 8:
Lower bound on
\bar{g}_{4}
as a function of
\bar{f}_{2}
. Colored dots represent the numericsl data obtained with
L_{\text{max}}
defined in (
60
) and various values of
N_{\text{max}}
. Black bars represent the extrapolation of the numerical results to
N_{\text{max}}\rightarrow\infty
and indicate the estimated errors.

Performing the above extrapolation in
L_{\text{max}}
and
N_{\text{max}}
is computationally very expensive since it requires obtaining a lot of numerical data. Let us explore a cheaper alternative. One could compute bounds at various values of
N_{\text{max}}
with

L_{\text{max}}=aN_{\text{max}}-b,

(59)

with
a
and
b
some integer numbers. Once the data with this
L_{\text{max}}
is obtained, we can perform a linear extrapolation in
1/N_{\text{max}}
to
N_{\text{max}}\rightarrow\infty
. In figure
7
we make several choices of
a
and
b
. All the resulting extrapolations lie very close to the one obtained by using the previous approach. Notice, however, that the choice of
a
and
b
cannot be completely arbitrary, the values of
L_{\text{max}}
should always be inside the plateau. In figure
7
we also indicate the extrapolation using the points on the plateau right before the divergence. We denote this choice by “End of plateaus”.
In the rest of this section, we shall use

L_{\text{max}}=5N_{\text{max}}-50\,,

(60)

to perform extrapolations to
N_{\text{max}}\rightarrow\infty
. From now on, we assume that (
60
) is a good choice for other values of
\bar{f}_{2}
and all the other bounds computed below.

We are finally in a position to present our lower bound on
\bar{g}_{4}
as a function of
\bar{f}_{2}
. It is given in figure
8
. The numerical data for different
N_{\text{max}}
is depicted by colored dots. The extrapolated values are given by black bars which also reflect a rough error of the extrapolation.
17
17

17

To estimate the error on the extrapolated bound, we perform a linear interpolation using
N_{\text{max}}\geq N_{*}
where
18\leq N_{*}\leq 24
and compute the maximal difference between interpolated values. Therefore, this error indicates if the linear interpolation is a reasonable extrapolation of the bound to
N_{\text{max}}\to\infty
.
Figure
2
presented in the introduction is obtained from
8
by using only the extrapolated results and connecting the points.

The lower bound on
\bar{g}_{4}
is symmetric under
\bar{f}_{2}\to-\bar{f}_{2}
as a consequence of the
\Phi_{2}\leftrightarrow-\Phi_{2}
symmetry in the unitarity constraints (
21
) when
\Phi_{5}=0
. Indeed, we observe that the optimal solution leads to
\Phi_{5}\approx 0
and thus the numerical solution is
\Phi_{2}\leftrightarrow-\Phi_{2}
symmetric. The same remains true in subsections
3.4.2
and
3.4.3
. The absolute minimum of the bound in figure
2
is achieved at
f_{2}=0
and is given by

\bar{g}_{4}\geq 0.0138\pm 0.0003.

(61)

For larger values of
\bar{f}_{2}
the errors quickly increase. In order to understand this, recall from subsection
3.2
that the boundary values
\bar{f}_{2}=\pm 1
are saturated by free theories with
g_{2}=0
. Moreover, we shall prove in subsection
4.3
that free theories are in fact the only theories with
\bar{f}_{2}=\pm 1
. In order to efficiently obtain bounds in this region we would need to keep
g_{2}\approx 0
, while in the present section we fix
g_{2}=100
. This leads to bad convergence when we approach
\bar{f}_{2}=\pm 1
.

vs.

and

at two fixed values

and

are shown in figures

and

. The colored lines indicate the numerical data for various values of

. The gray line indicates the extrapolated bound. The width of this line represents the extrapolation error.

As we can see from figures

and

N_{\text{max}}
one and lies very close to it. In what follows we will focus on

and

in order to reduce the cost of our numerical computation. This allows us to scan over more values of
\bar{f}_{2}
. In figures

we present the lower bound on
\bar{g}_{4}
at

. Using the

.

-

satisfy the naive condition

\bar{g}_{4}\pm 2\bar{f}_{4}\geq 0

which is obtained from (

3.5
Linear constraints and the numerical bounds

Let us now make a general remark on a limitation specific to convex optimization, when it comes to bounding observables like the ones in (
12
). For simplicity, consider the simplified setting of a single amplitude
\mathcal{T}(s)
, function of a single Mandelstam invariant—for instance, a scalar 2-to-2 amplitude in two dimensions. Given an amplitude which satisfies unitarity and crossing, it is easy to see that the one parameter family

\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}(s)=\lambda\mathcal{T}(s)~,\qquad\lambda\in[0,1]~,

(63)

does as well. This simple fact implies that
no two-sided bound is possible for dimensionless ratios of coefficients of different mass dimension, if they appear linearly in the amplitude.

Concretely, suppose that the amplitude has a low energy expansion of the form

\mathcal{T}(s)=g_{2}s^{2}+g_{3}s^{3}+\dots~.

(64)

Then, equation (
63
) generates a one-parameter family of allowed values for the dimensionless ratio:

\bar{g}_{3}(\lambda)=\frac{g_{3}(\lambda)}{{[g_{2}(\lambda)]}^{3/2}}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{1/2}}\frac{g_{3}}{g_{2}^{3/2}}~.

(65)

If
g_{3}
is positive (negative) in the original solution to unitarity and crossing,
\bar{g}_{3}
is unbounded from above (below).

It is worth emphasizing that the argument above is not sufficient to forbid two-sided bounds in physical EFTs: elastic unitarity imposes that Wilson coefficients appear nonlinearly in the low energy expansion—see (
6
) - (
8
). Hence, if particle production happens at some higher order in
s
, the reasoning must be modified. As mentioned in subsection
3.3
, we did add nonlinear terms in the ansatz, in particular involving
g_{2}
, with the exception of the runs to bound
\bar{f}_{2}
. However, as a matter of fact, in our numerical runs we only found two-sided bounds precisely for
\bar{f}_{2}
. The latter is a ratio of coefficients with the same mass dimension, and does not scale with the parameter
\lambda
.

This raises the question whether the absence of two-sided bounds is indeed a hallmark of Wilson coefficients of any EFT. In a certain region of parameter space, the answer is affirmative, as we shall see in detail in the next section. Indeed, weakly coupled theories precisely come equipped with a small parameter
\lambda
, and the low energy Wilson coefficients are linear in it at leading order—see table
9
. Elastic unitarity is perturbatively satified order by order in
\lambda
, hence one can consider these theories—if they can be UV completed—as a non-linear completion of the example in (
63
).
We will indeed use weakly coupled theories to explain some features of the plots presented in this section.

3.6
Numerical tests of low spin dominance

In this subsection, we test low spin dominance which was conjectured in
Bern:2021ppb
.
18
18

18

See also
Chowdhury:2021ynh
;
Ghosh:2022net
where low spin dominance was shown to follow from locality in tree level EFTs.
Consider the observables

\displaystyle\langle\rho_{\ell}^{+}\rangle_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv 16(\ell+1)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\text{Im}\left(\Phi_{1}^{\ell}(s)+\Phi_{2}^{\ell}(s)\right)}{s^{k+1}}ds\,,

(66)

\displaystyle\langle\rho_{\ell}^{-}\rangle_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv 16(\ell+1)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\text{Im}\left(\Phi_{1}^{\ell}(s)-\Phi_{2}^{\ell}(s)\right)}{s^{k+1}}ds\,,

\displaystyle\langle\rho_{\ell}^{3}\rangle_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv 16(\ell+1)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\text{Im}\left(\Phi_{3}^{\ell}(s)\right)}{s^{k+1}}ds\,.

In our case, only the
k=2
integrals converge because for higher values of
k
, the logarithms in (
6
) spoil convergence. Note that in terms of these positive observables, the sum rules (
37
) can be written (after taking the limit
\hat{s}\rightarrow
0 there) as

\displaystyle g_{2}+f_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0,2\ldots}\langle\rho_{\ell}^{+}\rangle_{2}+\sum_{l=2,3\ldots}\langle\rho_{\ell}^{3}\rangle_{2}\,,

(67)

\displaystyle g_{2}-f_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0,2\ldots}\langle\rho_{\ell}^{-}\rangle_{2}+\sum_{l=2,3\ldots}\langle\rho_{\ell}^{3}\rangle_{2}\,.

The weak version of low spin dominance states that in each channel, the contribution of the lowest spin dominates over all the higher spins i.e.

\frac{\langle\rho_{0}^{+}\rangle_{2}}{\langle\rho_{\ell>0}^{+}\rangle_{2}}\geq 1,\quad\frac{\langle\rho_{0}^{-}\rangle_{2}}{\langle\rho_{\ell>0}^{-}\rangle_{2}}\geq 1,\quad\frac{\langle\rho_{0}^{3}\rangle_{2}}{\langle\rho_{\ell>0}^{3}\rangle_{2}}\geq 1\,.

(68)

The strong version of low spin dominance instead states that in fact the contribution from the lowest spin is much larger than the other spins

\frac{\langle\rho_{0}^{+}\rangle_{2}}{\langle\rho_{\ell>0}^{+}\rangle_{2}}\geq\alpha,\quad\frac{\langle\rho_{0}^{-}\rangle_{2}}{\langle\rho_{\ell>0}^{-}\rangle_{2}}\geq\alpha,\quad\frac{\langle\rho_{0}^{3}\rangle_{2}}{\langle\rho_{\ell>0}^{3}\rangle_{2}}\geq\alpha\,,

(69)

with
\alpha\sim 100
in
Bern:2021ppb
. In our work, since we construct scattering amplitudes, we test this conjecture and find that the weak version of low spin dominance seems to always be true, however the strong version need not be. More precisely we find amplitudes where
\alpha<10
. We illustrate this by considering two points from figure
10
in tables
5
and
6
. Note that in this subsection we analyzed the data at
N_{\text{max}}=26
and
L_{\text{max}}=80
and checked that the behavior is stable as we change
L_{\text{max}}
and
N_{\text{max}}
. In table
5
which is the absolute minimum value of
\bar{g}_{4}
, we see that
19
19

19

Note that when
\bar{f}_{2}=0
and
\bar{f}_{4}=0
, the amplitude
\Phi_{2}=0
and therefore
\Phi^{-}_{\ell}=\Phi^{+}_{\ell}
.

\bar{f}_{2}=0\,,\bar{f}_{4}=0\,:\quad\frac{\langle\rho_{0}^{+}\rangle_{2}}{\langle\rho_{\ell>0}^{+}\rangle_{2}}=5.7,\quad\quad\frac{\langle\rho_{0}^{3}\rangle_{2}}{\langle\rho_{\ell>0}^{3}\rangle_{2}}=6.3\,,

(70)

which indicates some low spin dominance but not the strong form. We contrast this in table
6
with the point
\bar{f}_{2}=0
and
\bar{f}_{4}=0.05
which lies to right hand side of the minimum where the bound between
\bar{f}_{4}
and
\bar{g}_{4}
appears to be linear. Here we observe much stronger low spin dominance, albeit only in the
\rho_{\ell}^{+}
channel:

\bar{f}_{2}=0\,,\bar{f}_{4}=0.05\,:\quad\frac{\langle\rho_{0}^{+}\rangle_{2}}{\langle\rho_{\ell>0}^{+}\rangle_{2}}=21.9,\quad\frac{\langle\rho_{0}^{-}\rangle_{2}}{\langle\rho_{\ell>0}^{-}\rangle_{2}}=3.3,\quad\frac{\langle\rho_{0}^{3}\rangle_{2}}{\langle\rho_{\ell>0}^{3}\rangle_{2}}=8.8\,.

(71)

\bar{f}_{2}=0

,

and
f_{2}=30
.

4
Allowed amplitudes from perturbation theory

In the first part of this work, we saw how to explore the space of Wilson coefficients using the numerical S-matrix bootstrap. This section is dedicated to answering the following question: how much of this space can be understood analytically? Our main tool will be a set of effective field theories which are under perturbative control, and which will be used to populate the space of Wilson coefficients.
Most of the models we consider were shown in
Henriksson:2021ymi
to be compatible with analytic positivity bounds, however we also discuss two cases which are in tension with the same bounds. These theories are obtained by weakly coupling the photon to a heavy particle at tree level (Yukawa-like theories) or at one loop (QED-like theories). The resulting theories have the following feature: they all have a dimensionless parameter
\lambda
, such that at the point
\lambda=0
the heavy particle decouples and the photons are free. These theories are not necessarily UV complete on their own when
\lambda\neq 0
, but for some of them one can exhibit explicit UV completions.
20
20

20

More precisely, what one can concoct is a renormalizable action with marginal couplings. This still leaves open the possibility of a Landau pole, see appendix
F
.
Furthermore, they all obey the classical Regge growth conjecture
Chowdhury:2019kaq
, which states that the Regge intercept should be bounded by two for tree-level scattering amplitudes. The non-perturbative version of the bound is not known in the case of massless spin one particles, but the same statement was recently proven for graviton scattering
Haring:2022cyf
, and we shall assume that it holds for photons as well. Even when we do not exhibit an explicit UV completion, we take the Regge boundedness as a reason to trust in its existence.

The rest of the section is organized as follows. In subsection
4.1
, we gather the Wilson coefficients of interest, while a detailed analysis of the theories that produce them is relegated to appendix
E
. The following subsection is dedicated to explaining which linear combinations of the basis of amplitudes are compatible with unitarity. In the three subsections
4.3
,
4.4
,
4.5
, we rule in portions of the space of physical observables defined in (
12
).

4.1
Wilson coefficients of models with a small parameter

As mentioned above, the class of theories we consider are obtained by integrating out a particle at tree level or at one-loop. At tree level, we consider resonances of spin bounded by two. The criterion for this choice is the requirement of Regge boundedness discussed above. In appendix
E
we give more details on the construction of these amplitudes, and in subsections
4.3
,
4.4
we will further comment on the case of a spin two resonance, which leads to some subtleties. The second class of theories comprises the coupling of a photon to an electrically charged particle: the particle is only created in pairs, and therefore contributes to the photon EFT at one loop. We collect the Wilson coefficients in table
9
, where
\lambda
is a dimensionless parameter. In all cases, the coefficients are only correct to leading order in
\lambda
, which should therefore be taken to be small. In the following, we shall often denote the couplings by the dimension of the corresponding operators in the Lagrangian (
13
):

(g_{2},f_{2})
are the dimension 8 Wilson coefficients,
(g_{3},f_{3},h_{3})
are the dimension 10 and
(g_{4},g_{4}^{\prime},f_{4})
are the dimension 12 coefficients.

4.2
The rules of the game

If the amplitudes in table
9
are to be treated as UV complete, their convex hull should lie within the numerical bounds found in section
3
. However, violations of unitarity only disappear in the
\lambda\to 0
limit, in which case all the dimensionless observables defined in (
12
) are pushed to infinity, with the exception of
\bar{f_{2}}
. For instance, the non-vanishing observables associated with the first row of table
9
are

\bar{f}_{2}=1~,\quad\bar{g}_{3}=\frac{1}{\lambda}~,\quad\bar{f}_{3}=\frac{3}{\lambda}~,\quad\bar{g}_{4}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}~,\quad\bar{f}_{4}=\frac{1}{2\lambda^{2}}~.

(73)

We conclude that the amplitudes considered in this section mark
unbounded directions
in the space of photon EFTs at dimension larger than 8.

In the rest of this section, we shall find such directions. A simplifying feature of the problem is that the convex hull of these amplitudes degenerates into a cone in the weak coupling limit. Indeed, let us define the vectors
v_{i}(\lambda_{i})
, where the index
i
labels the rows of table
9
and whose components are the Wilson coefficients:

v=(g_{2},f_{2}\,|\,g_{3},f_{3},h_{3}\,|\,g_{4},g^{\prime}_{4},f_{4})=(v^{(2)}\,|\,v^{(3)}\,|\,v^{(4)})~.

(74)

The vectors spanning subspaces at each mass dimension are distinguished by the upper index.
The following obvious equality,

\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}v_{i}(\lambda_{i})=\sum_{i}\beta_{i}v_{i}(\lambda^{\prime}_{i})~,\qquad\beta_{i}=\frac{\alpha_{i}}{(\kappa_{i})^{2}}~,\quad\lambda^{\prime}_{i}=\lambda_{i}\kappa_{i}~,\quad\kappa_{i}>0~,

(75)

makes the condition
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}=1
irrelevant. In the
\lambda_{i}\to 0
limit, all linear combinations of the amplitudes with coefficients
\beta_{i}>0
are allowed. In other words, perturbative amplitudes are only constrained by positivity, rather than by full nonlinear unitarity.

A second simplification comes from the fact that we are allowed to take
independent
linear combinations at dimension 8 and 10, or at dimension 8 and 12. This follows from the freedom of choosing the mass of the exchanged particle, which introduces new dimensionless parameters
m_{i}/m_{j}
. For instance, the following change of variables,

x=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{4}}~,\quad y=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{6}}~,

(76)

allows to write the Wilson coefficients at dimension 8 and 10 as linear functions of different parameters. Referring to the definition (
74
),

v^{(2)}=x\,\hat{v}^{(2)}~,\quad v^{(3)}=y\,\hat{v}^{(3)}~,\quad v^{(4)}=\frac{y^{2}}{x}\hat{v}^{(4)}~,

(77)

with hatted vectors being purely numerical. Then we can extend the observation (
75
) to

\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}v_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})=\sum_{i}\left(\beta_{i}^{(2)}v_{i}^{(2)}(x^{\prime}_{i})\,|\,\beta_{i}^{(3)}v_{i}^{(3)}(y^{\prime}_{i})\,|\,\beta^{(4)}_{i}v_{i}^{(4)}(y^{\prime 2}_{i}/x^{\prime}_{i})\right)~,\\
\beta_{i}^{(2)}=\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\kappa^{(2)}_{i}}~,\ \beta_{i}^{(3)}=\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\kappa^{(3)}_{i}}~,\ \beta_{i}^{(4)}=\alpha_{i}\frac{\kappa^{(2)}_{i}}{\big(\kappa^{(3)}_{i}\big)^{2}}~,\qquad x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{i}\kappa^{(2)}_{i}~,\ y^{\prime}_{i}=y_{i}\kappa^{(3)}_{i}~,\quad\kappa_{i}^{(2,3)}>0~.

(78)

This means that, for any choice of positive
\beta_{i}^{(2)},\,\beta_{i}^{(3)}
, one can find an amplitude in the convex hull of the perturbative theories defined by table
9
as
\lambda\to 0.
21
21

21

Notice that instead
\beta_{i}^{(4)}=\big(\beta_{i}^{(3)}\big)^{2}/\beta_{i}^{(2)}.
An analogous statement holds for the subspace of dimension 8 and dimension 12 coefficients.

A simple consequence of this observation is that
the asymptotic allowed values of the dimension 10 or of the dimension 12 coefficients do not depend on
\bar{f_{2}}
.

More generally, we are lead to a simple recipe to find which directions are necessarily unbounded in the space of couplings of each mass dimension:

Consider the vectors of Wilson coefficients in table
9
, restricted to a given mass dimension larger than 8. Set
\lambda=m=1
. Compute the one-sided cone generated by positive linear combinations of the numerical vectors thus obtained. The intersection of this cone with the sphere at infinity marks the unbounded directions.

The basic technology to solve this kind of problems is reviewed in
Arkani-Hamed:2020blm
. We shall only report the results in the rest of the section.

4.3
The
\bar{f}_{2}
space

The spin 0 exchanges in table
9
are sufficient to cover the space of couplings at dimension 8, which is parametrized by
|\bar{f_{2}}|\leq 1
. Indeed, the scalar amplitude has
\bar{f_{2}}=1
and the axion amplitude has
\bar{f_{2}}=-1
. Their convex hull generates the whole allowed space.

The other amplitudes in table
9
all lie inside this interval. In the case of the spin 2 resonance, this fact deserves a comment. The exchange of a resonance determines the amplitude uniquely only at the location of the pole. In particular, the amplitudes constructed in appendix
E
are ambiguous up to the addition of a polynomial in the Mandelstam invariants, of the kind showed in (
6
). Such polynomials correspond to contact interactions for the photons, as explained in the introduction. A polynomial of degree two can be tuned so that the Regge limit of the spin two resonance obeys (
33
), and, as a consequence, the dispersion relation (
37
). This implies
|\bar{f}_{2}|\leq 1
. Let us emphasize that (
33
) is strictly weaker than the Regge bound mentioned in the previous subsection, since it only holds at
t=0
, for a specific linear combination of amplitudes. In fact, it is not possible to obtain a Regge intercept strictly less than 2, for all values of
t
, by adding a finite degree polynomial.

Let us finally comment on the constraints imposed by dispersion relations and crossing onto the amplitudes living at the boundary of the allowed region. We start from the boundary
\bar{f_{2}}=1
. Equations (
37
) and (
28
) imply

\operatorname{Im}\left(\Phi_{1}(s)-\Phi_{2}(s)\right)=0~,\qquad\operatorname{Im}\Phi_{3}(s)=0~,

(79)

where we recall that the notation means the amplitudes are evaluated at
t=0
. Let us now decompose (
79
) in partial waves, using (
121
). The small Wigner
d-
matrix are non negative at
\theta=0
, as it is easy to verify from the definition (
119
). Together with the positivity constraint (
25
), this means that (
79
) is valid at the level of partial waves:

\operatorname{Im}\left(\Phi_{1}^{\ell}(s)-\Phi_{2}^{\ell}(s)\right)=0~,\qquad\operatorname{Im}\Phi_{3}^{\ell}(s)=0~.

(80)

Furthermore, the unitarity constraints (
136
) - (
139
) force these combinations of partial waves to vanish:

\left(\Phi_{1}^{\ell}(s)-\Phi_{2}^{\ell}(s)\right)=0~,\qquad\Phi_{3}^{\ell}(s)=0~.

(81)

This is easily seen for the
\Phi_{1}^{\ell}(s)-\Phi_{2}^{\ell}(s)
combination, which obeys the usual spinless unitarity constraint
|1+i\mathcal{T}|<1
.
22
22

22

Recall that
t-u
symmetry of
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
and the property
d^{\ell}_{0,0}(\pi-\theta)=(-1)^{\ell}d^{\ell}_{0,0}(\theta)
make
\Phi_{1}^{\ell}(s)-\Phi_{2}^{\ell}(s)
vanish for
\ell
odd.
With some more work, one can extract from (
139
) the following necessary conditions valid for even spin:

\left|1+i(\Phi_{1}^{\ell}(s)+\Phi_{2}^{\ell}(s))\right|<1~,\qquad\left|1+2i\Phi_{3}^{\ell}(s)\right|<1~,\qquad\left|\Phi_{5}^{\ell}(s)\right|<1~.

(82)

The second of these conditions, together with (
137
), implies the third equality in (
81
).
23
23

23

Recall that
\Phi_{3}^{\ell}=0
for
\ell=0,\,1
.

If we now plug (
81
) back into (
139
), we discover that also

\Phi_{5}^{\ell}(s)=0~.

(83)

All in all, we found that the following amplitudes vanish if
\bar{f_{2}}=1
:

\Phi_{1}(s,t,u)-\Phi_{2}(s,t,u)=0~,\qquad\Phi_{3}(s,t,u)=0~,\qquad\Phi_{5}(s,t,u)=0~.

(84)

Using crossing—equation (
5
)—we conclude that in fact all the amplitudes must vanish. A similar reasoning also leads to the same conclusion for the other boundary of the space allowed by unitarity,
\bar{f_{2}}=-1
.

Notice that this conclusion does not contradict the statement that scalar and pseudoscalar resonances saturate these bounds. Indeed, as emphasized, the theories considered in this section are only compatible with unitarity in the limit
\lambda\to 0
,
i.e.
precisely when they become free. In other words, in any interacting UV completion of the EFT obtained integrating out a scalar or an axion, the value of
\bar{f_{2}}
is corrected at higher orders in
\lambda
.

4.4
The
(\bar{g}_{3},\bar{f}_{3},\bar{h}_{3})
space

In subsection
3.3
, we saw that the numerics indicates the absence of bounds for the dimension 10 observables. Here, we show that the full space is in fact covered by (linear combinations of) perturbative completions of the photon effective lagrangian. Following the recipe given in subsection
4.2
, we consider the cone generated by the following amplitudes: scalar, axion, scalar QED, spinor QED and vector QED. One can easily see that the cone coincides with the whole three-dimensional space spanned by
g_{3}
,
f_{3}
and
h_{3}
, thus proving that the sphere at infinity in the normalized space
(\bar{g}_{3},\bar{f}_{3},\bar{h}_{3})
is populated by weakly coupled theories.

Notice that, since the unitarity constraints are convex, no lower bound on
\bar{g}_{3}
,
\bar{f}_{3}
,
\bar{h}_{3}
is possible either. Let us emphasize that it was not necessary to include spin two resonances in order to achieve this result. In other words, bounds on dimension 10 Wilson coefficients are impossible already within amplitudes with Regge intercept smaller than two.

4.5
The
(\bar{g}_{4},\bar{g}^{\prime}_{4},\bar{f}_{4})
space

The space of dimension 12 Wilson coefficients is the first one which is not fully covered by (linear combinations of) weakly coupled amplitudes. This is in accordance with the results of section
3
, where we presented evidence of non-trivial bounds in the
(\bar{g}_{4},\bar{g}^{\prime}_{4},\bar{f}_{4})
space.

The boundaries of the cone generated by the perturbative amplitudes depend on which portion of table
9
is included in the analysis. Let us begin by including all the rows of the table. These vectors generate a cone bounded by the following four faces:

g_{4}-2f_{4}\geq 0~,\qquad g_{4}+2f_{4}\geq 0~,\qquad g_{4}^{\prime}\leq 0~,\qquad g_{4}^{\prime}+6g_{4}\geq 0~.

(85)

This implies in particular
g_{4}\geq 0
. If instead we restrict to theories whose Regge intercept is strictly less than two,
i.e.
we exclude the spin two resonances, we get the following inequalities:

-10f_{4}+5g_{4}+2g_{4}^{\prime}\geq 0~,\qquad-157f_{4}+85g_{4}+60g_{4}^{\prime}\geq 0~,\qquad g_{4}^{\prime}\leq 0~,\qquad 230f_{4}+115g_{4}+94g_{4}^{\prime}\geq 0~.

(86)

Again, only the half-space
g_{4}\geq 0
is populated. The two cones are shown in figure
17
.

Figure 17:
A portion of the cone generated by positive linear combinations of the perturbative amplitudes of table
9
, restricted to the space of dimension 12 Wilson coefficients. The smaller cone (yellow) only includes amplitudes which grow slower than
s^{2}
in the Regge limit. The wider cone (purple) includes all the rows of table
9
.

It is interesting to compare these findings with the amplitudes constructed numerically in section
3
, and with the analytic results obtained in
Arkani-Hamed:2020blm
;
Henriksson:2021ymi
;
Henriksson:2022oeu
. The numerical results are showcased in figures
10
-
12
and
14
-
16
, for the
(\bar{g}_{4},\bar{f}_{4})
and for the
(\bar{g}_{4},\bar{g}^{\prime}_{4})
planes, respectively. In particular, it is clear that the
(\bar{g}_{4},\bar{f}_{4})
space is bounded at infinity by the two directions
\bar{g}_{4}/\bar{f}_{4}=\pm 2
, for all values of
\bar{f}_{2}
. This matches both the wider and the narrower cones, equations (
85
) and (
86
). The two boundaries are generated by the scalar and axion amplitudes respectively (and also by the parity even I and by the parity odd amplitudes) in table
9
.

The status of the
(\bar{g}_{4},\bar{g}^{\prime}_{4})
plane appears less clear. The bounds in figures
14
-
16
are well converged only when
\bar{g}^{\prime}_{4}\gtrsim 0
, while all the perturbative amplitudes we consider have non-positive values for the same Wilson coefficient. The two plots are therefore hardly comparable.
It would be interesting to find a perturbative amplitude with positive
g_{4}^{\prime}
that could explain our numerical results.
For reference, the sections of the two cones in (
85
) and (
86
) are bounded, respectively, as

-6\leq\frac{g_{4}^{\prime}}{g_{4}}\leq 0~,\qquad g_{4}\geq 0~,

(87)

and

-\frac{345}{262}\leq\frac{g_{4}^{\prime}}{g_{4}}\leq 0~,\qquad g_{4}\geq 0~.

(88)

The lower bound in the first equation is reached by the parity even I and parity odd spin 2 resonances. If those are excluded, smallest ratio belongs to vector QED. One can also combine the numerical results to the weakly coupled theories to obtain a larger convex hull. In particular, one can consider figure
14
, and draw a line with slope
-1/6
or
-262/345
(depending on the reader’s opinion about the spin 2 resonances) which intersects the
\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}=0
axis in correspondence of the numerical lower bound. The region to the right of this line and above the bound is populated by linear combinations of numerical and perturbative amplitudes.

Finally, in the
(\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime},\bar{f}_{4})
plane, the perturbative amplitudes cover the semi-circle at infinity with
\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}<0
. Therefore, their convex hull covers the whole half-plane
(\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}<0,\bar{f}_{4})
. Again, one can combine this knowledge with the numerical results. From figures
14
and
14
it is clear that there is no upper bound on
\bar{g}^{\prime}_{4}
.
Consider then an allowed point with
\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}=x>0
in the
(\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime},\bar{f}_{4})
plane. The convex hull of this point and the half plane
\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}<0
is the region to the left of the line
\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}=x
. Now, since
x
can go to infinity, we conclude that there are no bounds in the
(\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime},\bar{f}_{4})
plane.

In
Henriksson:2021ymi
;
Henriksson:2022oeu
, the space of Wilson coefficients was bounded analytically, by means of linear positivity—see section
2
—and tree level crossing,
i.e.
null constraints. Depending on how many null constraints were imposed, the following bounds were found:

\displaystyle-\frac{18}{7}\leq\frac{\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}}{\bar{g}_{4}}\leq\frac{370}{29}~,\quad g_{4}\geq 0~,

\displaystyle\textup{\cite[cite]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{Henriksson:2021ymi}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}}~,

(89)

\displaystyle-\frac{12}{5}\leq\frac{\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}}{\bar{g}_{4}}\leq 0~,\quad g_{4}\geq 0~,

\displaystyle\textup{\cite[cite]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{Henriksson:2022oeu}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}}~.

(90)

On the other hand, the paper
Arkani-Hamed:2020blm
found the following bounds:

-6\leq\frac{\bar{g}_{4}^{\prime}}{\bar{g}_{4}}\leq\frac{30}{7}~,\quad g_{4}\geq 0~.

(91)

Figure 18:
Comparison between (
87
), (
88
)—purple and orange solid lines respectively—and the analytic bounds imposed by linear positivity and tree level crossing. The allowed region (a) was derived in
Arkani-Hamed:2020blm
, (b) in
Henriksson:2021ymi
and updated by the same authors in
Henriksson:2022oeu
(c). Notice that part of the purple region lies outside the bounds which rely on null constraints.

Figure 19:
Comparison between the numerical bounds obtained in section
3
and the analytic bounds (a)
Arkani-Hamed:2020blm
, (b)
Henriksson:2021ymi
and (c)
Henriksson:2022oeu
. The allowed regions lie in the
\bar{g}_{4}>0
half-plane, and are delimited by the dashed lines (notice that the blue and green left edges almost coincide in the picture).

The bounds (
90
) - (
91
) are compared to the weakly coupled theories (
87
) and (
88
) in figure
18
, and to the numerical results in figure
19
. It is interesting to notice that the cone generated by our perturbative amplitudes extends beyond the bounds found in
Henriksson:2021ymi
;
Henriksson:2022oeu
. This is not a contradiction, since the use of null constraints requires the Regge limit to be strictly softer than
s^{2}
beyond the forward limit. Indeed, excluding the spin 2 exchanges we get a convex hull squarely contained within their bounds. However,
Henriksson:2021ymi
did consider the exchange of a massive graviton, finding it compatible with the analytic bounds. As we further discuss in appendix
E
, the amplitude proposed in
Henriksson:2021ymi
coincides with our parity even II up to operators of dimension 8 in the EFT. One can easily check that the Wilson coefficients of dimension 10 and 12 in table
9
coincide with the ones reported in table 2 of
Henriksson:2021ymi
. At the level of our analysis, which is based on the classical Regge bound, there is no reason to prefer this combination to the other two spin 2 resonances: it would be interesting to understand if, in some way, the other amplitudes are exonerated from the null constraints of
Henriksson:2021ymi
;
Henriksson:2022oeu
, while this one must obey them. In appendix
E
, we offer a few additional comments on the status of the parity even I and parity odd EFTs.

5
Discussion

Photons are the particles of light. To the best of our knowledge, they are massless spin one particles. Assuming four dimensional Lorentz invariance, quantum mechanics and the absence of other massless particles, the low energy dynamics of photons must be described by the EFT Lagrangian (
13
). Of course, we expect photons to also interact with gravitons, but this is a excindingly small effect at reasonable energies.
24
24

24

For example, the exclusive cross section of two photons into two photons from 1-loop effects in QED is of order
\sigma_{QED}\sim\alpha^{4}s^{3}/m_{e}^{8}
and from tree-level graviton exchange is of order
\sigma_{grav}\sim G_{N}^{2}s\sim s/m_{P}^{4}
. Therefore, the ratio
\sigma_{grav}/\sigma_{QED}\sim m_{e}^{8}/(m_{P}^{4}\alpha^{4}s^{2})\sim 10^{-58}\left(1\,eV/\sqrt{s}\right)^{4}
is tiny for visible (or higher frequency) photons.

The Wilson coefficients
c_{i}
in the EFT Lagrangian (
13
)
parametrize our ignorance about the high energy behavior of the theory.
Of course, in the real world we expect these to be dominated by QED effects. Nevertheless, in this paper we asked ourselves what values can these numbers take compatible with the S-matrix bootstrap principles of Lorentz invariance, unitarity and analyticity.
Using a numerical algorithm, we estimated several non-perturbative bounds as described in section
3
. QED seems to live well inside the allowed region.
Some bounds are saturated by weakly coupled amplitudes as we discuss in section
4
but others are not. For example, the amplitude with minimal
\bar{g}_{4}
is strongly coupled. Is there a physical theory that realizes such a small value of

\bar{g}_{4}
? This is an open question for the future.

Let us comment on the bounds derived in
Henriksson:2021ymi
using positivity.
In that work, the authors assumed weak coupling below the scale
M
and neglected the branch cuts of the amplitude from photon loops. More precisely, they assumed the analytic structure depicted in figure
20
and defined the scale
M
from the position of the branch point at
s=M^{2}
. This assumption makes it possible to derive bounds on new dimensionless quantities like

\frac{g_{3}M^{2}}{g_{2}},\qquad\frac{g_{4}M^{4}}{g_{2}},\qquad\frac{g_{4}^{\prime}M^{4}}{g_{2}},\qquad\ldots

(92)

It would be interesting to derive similar bounds without completely ignoring the
\log
terms in (
6
).
