[ij]\to t_{i}t_{j}[ij],\qquad\langle ij\rangle\to t_{i}^{-1}t_{j}^{-1}\langle ij\rangle.

(240)

We can construct tensor structures
\mathbf{T}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}
in (
236
) as products of Lorentz invariant blocks (
237
). The exponents in these products are fixed by requiring correct transformation properties of the amplitude (
238
). In our case of identical spin one massless particles we can write for instance

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{+,+,+,+}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=([12][34])^{2}+([13][24])^{2}+([14][23])^{2},

(241)

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{+,+,-,-}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=([12]\langle 34\rangle)^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{+,-,+,-}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=([13]\langle 24\rangle)^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{+,-,-,+}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=([14]\langle 23\rangle)^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{+,+,+,-}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=(\langle 41\rangle[12][13])^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{+,+,-,+}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=(\langle 31\rangle[12][14])^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{+,-,+,+}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=(\langle 21\rangle[13][14])^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{-,+,+,+}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=(\langle 12\rangle[23][24])^{2}.

Due to the transformation properties (
240
) square blocks take care of positive helicities and angular blocks take care of negative helicities.

In (
241
) we have defined tensor structures for 8 helicity configurations. The other 8 configurations which have opposite helicities to (
241
) are obtained from (
241
) by exchanging square and angular blocks, namely

[ij]\leftrightarrow\langle ij\rangle.

(242)

It is interesting to notice that the following identity exists:

([12][34])^{2}+([13][24])^{2}+([14][23])^{2}=(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2})\frac{[12][34]}{\langle 12\rangle\langle 34\rangle}.

(243)

The tensor structure
\frac{[12][34]}{\langle 12\rangle\langle 34\rangle}
was used for example in
Arkani-Hamed:2020blm
. The latter choice might be more familiar to some readers.

B.4.2
Parity and permutation symmetry
S_{4}

We have constructed the basis of 16 tensor structures given by 8 structures written explicitly in (
241
) together with the 8 related ones (where all the helicities have an opposite sign) which are obtained from (
241
) as explained at the end of the last subsection. Plugging these 16 structures into (
236
) we obtain the final decomposition of the amplitude into tensor structures in spinor formalism.
The goal of this subsection is to impose the constraints of parity and permutation symmetry
S_{4}
on the decomposition (
236
) in order to get relations between the amplitude components
h_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4})}
.

Let us start with parity. According to equation (2.64) in
Hebbar:2020ukp
parity in our case requires

\mathcal{T}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}{}_{-\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{2},-\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}).

(244)

Since under parity the following holds

(\xi_{i})_{\alpha}\xleftrightarrow{\mathcal{P}}(\tilde{\xi}_{i})_{\dot{\alpha}},\qquad[ij]\xleftrightarrow{\mathcal{P}}\langle ij\rangle,

(245)

we conclude from (
236
) that

h_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4})}(s,t,u)=h_{(-\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{2},-\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{4})}(s,t,u).

(246)

We are thus left only with 8 independent functions
h_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4})}
.

Let us now impose the
S_{4}
permutation symmetry on the 8 amplitudes not related by parity. The amplitude which has all plus helicities is

\mathcal{T}_{+,+,+,+}.

(247)

It is obviously invariant under permutation of any particles. In particular

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{+,+,+,+}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}_{+,+,+,+}(p_{3},p_{2},p_{1},p_{4}),

(248)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{+,+,+,+}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}_{+,+,+,+}(p_{4},p_{2},p_{3},p_{1}).

(249)

From the explicit form of the
\mathbf{T}_{+,+,+,+}
tensor structure given in the first line of (
241
) we see that this tensor structure is also invariant under any permutations of particles, as a result we conclude that the function
h_{(+,+,+,+)}(s,t,u)
in (
236
) must be fully crossing symemtric in its arguments, namely

h_{(+,+,+,+)}(s,t,u)=h_{(+,+,+,+)}(t,s,u)=h_{(+,+,+,+)}(u,t,s).

(250)

Now consider amplitudes with two plus and two minus helicities, they are
38
38

38

Such amplitudes are often referred to in the literature as “Maximally Helicity Violating” (MHV) amplitudes.

\mathcal{T}_{+,+,-,-},\qquad\mathcal{T}_{+,-,+,-},\qquad\mathcal{T}_{+,-,-,+}.

(251)

Permutation symmetry relates them as

\mathcal{T}_{+,+,-,-}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}_{+,-,+,-}(p_{1},p_{3},p_{2},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}_{+,-,-,+}(p_{1},p_{3},p_{4},p_{2}).

(252)

Moreover, the first amplitude is also symmetric under both
1\leftrightarrow 2
and
3\leftrightarrow 4
permutations. (Similar statement can be made about the other two amplitudes in (
251
)). Investigating how the tensor structures in (
241
) transform under all these permutations we conclude that the following relations must be satisfied

\displaystyle h_{(++--)}(s,t,u)=h_{(++--)}(s,u,t)

\displaystyle=h_{(+-+-)}(t,s,u)=h_{(+-+-)}(t,u,s)

(253)

\displaystyle=h_{(+--+)}(u,t,s)=h_{(+--+)}(u,s,t).

Finally, let us consider the amplitudes with only one minus helicity, namely

\mathcal{T}_{+,+,+,-},\qquad\mathcal{T}_{+,+,-,+},\qquad\mathcal{T}_{+,-,+,+},\qquad\mathcal{T}_{-,+,+,+}.

(254)

They are all related by permutation symmetry

\mathcal{T}_{+,+,+,-}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}_{+,+,-,+}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{4},p_{3})=\\
\mathcal{T}_{+,-,+,+}(p_{1},p_{4},p_{2},p_{3})=\mathcal{T}_{-,+,+,+}(p_{4},p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}).

(255)

Moreover, the first amplitude in (
254
) is symmetric under any permutation of particles 1, 2 and
3
. (Similar statements hold for the rest of the amplitudes in (
254
)). Inspecting the permutation properties of the tensor structures in (
241
) we conclude that the following relations must be satisfied

h_{(+++-)}(s,t,u)=h_{(+++-)}(t,s,u)=h_{(+++-)}(u,t,s),

(256)

together with

h_{(+++-)}(s,t,u)=h_{(++-+)}(s,t,u)=h_{(+-++)}(s,t,u)=h_{(-+++)}(s,t,u).

(257)

In order to find this result notice, that the following relation holds due to momentum conservation

(\langle 41\rangle[12][13])^{2}=(\langle 42\rangle[12][23])^{2}=(\langle 43\rangle[32][13])^{2}.

(258)

Summarizing, out of 16 amplitude components
h_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4})}
there are only three independent ones. We denote them as

\displaystyle h_{1}(s|t,u)

\displaystyle\equiv h_{(++--)}(s,t,u),

(259)

\displaystyle h_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle\equiv(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2})^{-1}h_{(++++)}(s,t,u),

\displaystyle h_{3}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle\equiv h_{(+++-)}(s,t,u).

The rest of the functions
h_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4})}
can be obtained from these ones by using (
246
) which holds due to parity and the relations (
250
), (
253
), (
256
) and (
257
) which hold due to permutation symmetry
S_{4}
. Notice, that the functions
h_{2}
and
h_{3}
are fully crossing symmetric in their arguments, whereas the function
h_{1}(s|t,u)
is symmetric only in its last two arguments. The prefactor in the definition of
h_{2}
is introduced for later convenience.

B.4.3
Center of mass frame

We have worked so far with all-in amplitudes. Let us now use (
158
) in order to obtain in-out amplitudes from the all-in ones. Explicitly, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{++}_{++}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}_{++--}(p_{1},p_{2},-p_{3},-p_{4}),

(260)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{--}_{++}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}_{++++}(p_{1},p_{2},-p_{3},-p_{4}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{+-}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}_{+--+}(p_{1},p_{2},-p_{3},-p_{4}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{-+}_{+-}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}_{+-+-}(p_{1},p_{2},-p_{3},-p_{4}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{+-}_{++}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}_{++-+}(p_{1},p_{2},-p_{3},-p_{4}).

The amplitudes in the right-hand side can still be decomposed into tensor structures (
236
) with the basis given by (
241
). Evaluating (
260
) in the center of mass frame defined in (
104
) and using (
107
) together with (
236
), (
241
) and (
259
) we conclude that

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=([12]\langle 34\rangle)^{2}h_{1}(s|t,u)\Big|_{\text{COM}},

(261)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\left(([12][34])^{2}+([13][24])^{2}+([14][23])^{2}\right)(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2})^{-1}h_{2}(s,t,u)\Big|_{\text{COM}},

\displaystyle\Phi_{3}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=([14]\langle 23\rangle)^{2}h_{1}(u|t,s)\Big|_{\text{COM}},

\displaystyle\Phi_{4}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=([13]\langle 24\rangle)^{2}h_{1}(t|s,u)\Big|_{\text{COM}},

\displaystyle\Phi_{5}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=(\langle 31\rangle[12][14])^{2}h_{3}(s,t,u)\Big|_{\text{COM}}.

In order to evaluate the tensor structures in (
261
) in the center of mass frame we first apply the analytic continuation (
159
) to (
195
). We then plug in the center of mass frame values for the 4-momenta given in (
104
). This leads us to

\displaystyle\xi_{1}^{\text{COM}}

\displaystyle=s^{1/4}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\;\;\xi_{2}^{\text{COM}}=s^{1/4}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix},

(262)

\displaystyle\quad\xi_{3}^{\text{COM}}

\displaystyle=is^{1/4}\begin{pmatrix}\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\\
\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\end{pmatrix},\quad\xi_{4}^{\text{COM}}=is^{1/4}\begin{pmatrix}\sin\frac{\theta}{2}\\
-\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\end{pmatrix},

(263)

together with

\tilde{\xi}_{1}^{\text{COM}}=\xi_{1}^{\text{COM}},\qquad\tilde{\xi}_{2}^{\text{COM}}=\xi_{2}^{\text{COM}},\qquad\tilde{\xi}_{3}^{\text{COM}}=\xi_{3}^{\text{COM}},\qquad\tilde{\xi}_{4}^{\text{COM}}=\xi_{4}^{\text{COM}}.

(264)

Where the
i
in
\xi^{\text{COM}}_{3,4}
and
\tilde{\xi}^{\text{COM}}_{3,4}
comes from the analytic continuation from incoming to outgoing particle.

Plugging these into the definitions (
237
) we conclude

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=s^{2}h_{1}(s|t,u),

(265)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=h_{2}(s,t,u),

\displaystyle\Phi_{3}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=u^{2}h_{1}(u|t,s),

\displaystyle\Phi_{4}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=t^{2}h_{1}(t|s,u),

\displaystyle\Phi_{5}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=stu\,h_{3}(s,t,u).

The center of mass amplitudes
\Phi_{I}
are related to the amplitude components
\mathcal{A}_{I}
via (
156
) and (
211
). The amplitude components
\mathcal{A}_{I}
in turn are expressed in terms of the 3 functions
f_{i}
according to (
224
). Using (
265
) we can then relate
h_{i}
and
f_{i}
functions. We get

\displaystyle h_{1}(s|t,u)

\displaystyle=f_{1}(s|t,u)+\frac{1}{4}(f_{2}(s|t,u)+f_{2}(t|s,u)+f_{2}(u|s,t)),

(266)

\displaystyle h_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=s^{2}f_{1}(s|t,u)+t^{2}f_{1}(t|s,u)+u^{2}f_{1}(u|s,t)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}(tuf_{2}(s|t,u)+suf_{2}(t|s,u)+stf_{2}(u|s,t))+stuf_{3}(s,t,u),

\displaystyle h_{3}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}f_{3}(s,t,u).

Notice that the functions
h_{i}
written in terms of functions
f_{i}
here are invariant under the equivalence class transformation (
225
). This is a powerful consistency check.

Appendix C
One-loop from elastic unitarity

In this appendix, we will compute the one-loop amplitude at threshold using elastic unitarity. This computation follows the idea of
EliasMiro:2019kyf
;
Guerrieri:2020bto
;
Guerrieri:2021ivu
, the novelty compared to those references is the presence of spin and we will see that the general result can be easily extended.

The (tree-level) amplitudes are schematically given at threshold by (
6
)

\mathcal{T}=a_{2}\bar{s}^{2}+a_{3}\bar{s}^{3}+\mathcal{O}(\bar{s}^{4})\,,

(267)

where we wrote generic dimensionless coefficients
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and used the dimensionless Mandelstam variables (
53
). From unitarity (
\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{T}\sim\mathcal{T}^{2}
), we expect the amplitude to acquire an imaginary part at
\mathcal{O}(\bar{s}^{4})
. This discontinuity corresponds to EFT loops and, as we are considering massless particles, it is given at threshold by
logs
. This leads to the ansatz (
6
), with the one-loop discontinuity given by (
7
).

We will now show how to fix the coefficients
\beta_{i}
to (
8
) by imposing unitarity at threshold. To begin, we decompose the ansatz (
6
) in the partial waves amplitudes
\mathbb{S}^{\ell}(s)
, defined in (
22
).

Elastic unitarity is broken due to particle production starting at
\mathcal{T}(2\to 4)=\mathcal{O}(\bar{s}^{3})
. Therefore at threshold, unitarity implies

\text{Eigenvalues}\left[(\mathbb{S}^{\ell}(s))^{\dagger}\mathbb{S}^{\ell}(s)\right]=1+\mathcal{O}(\bar{s}^{6})\,.

(268)

We can expand the partial wave amplitude as follows:

\mathbb{S}^{\ell}(s)=\mathbb{I}+i(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\ell}\bar{s}^{2}+\mathbb{F}_{3}^{\ell}\bar{s}^{3}+\mathbb{F}_{4}^{\ell}\bar{s}^{4})+\mathcal{O}(\bar{s}^{4}\log\bar{s})\,,

(269)

and (
268
) implies

2\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{F}_{4}^{\ell}=(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\ell})^{2}\,,\quad\forall\ \ell\,.

(270)

We recall here that
\mathbb{F}^{\ell}
are one-by-one or two-by-two matrices (
22
). Imposing this equation allows us to fix the coefficients
\beta_{i}
using for the logarithm the prescription
\log(-s)=\log(|s|)-i\pi\theta(s)
. The result matches the explicit loop computation described in appendix
D
. Note that this result could be extended to
\mathcal{O}(\bar{s}^{5})
, as elastic unitarity is only broken at
\mathcal{O}(\bar{s}^{6})
(
268
).

Appendix D
Computation of scattering amplitudes in EFTs

In this appendix, we will compute scattering amplitudes from the general EFT written in (
13
) to eighth order in derivatives i.e. fourth order in
s
. This means that we must go to one-loop order in the dim-8 vertices
\mathcal{L}_{8}
given in (
14
) but only to linear order in the dim-10
\mathcal{L}_{10}
and dim-12
\mathcal{L}_{12}
vertices. The one-loop diagrams computed using dim-8 vertices will turn out to be UV-divergent and therefore we will need to regularize the integral and add counter terms to cancel these UV-divergences, using say the
\overline{MS}
prescription. The calculations in this section were done with the help of software packages FeynCalc
Mertig:1990an
;
Shtabovenko:2016sxi
;
Shtabovenko:2020gxv
, FeynArts
Hahn:2000kx
, FeynHelpers
Shtabovenko:2016whf
, FeynRules
Alloul:2013bka
and PackageX
Patel:2016fam
.
We now proceed order by order in derivatives or equivalently in
s
.

O(
s^{2}
):

This corresponds to tree level in the dimension 8 lagragian
\mathcal{L}_{8}
. Conceptually it is straightforward to compute the Feynman amplitude from the Lagrangian. However, on a technical level, the process is a bit tricky due to the various Lorentz indices and different possible contractions between them. Using computer algebra to perform the contractions, we arrive at

M_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=8c_{1}\eta_{\mu\nu}\eta_{\rho\sigma}s^{2}+4c_{2}{p_{4}}_{\mu}{p_{3}}_{\nu}\eta_{\rho\sigma}s+\ldots

(271)

where, for brevity, we only displayed a couple of terms. From this Feynman amplitude, one can derive the scattering amplitude by contracting the Lorentz indices with photon polarization vectors in the COM frame:

\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3}\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)=\epsilon_{\lambda_{1}}^{\mu}(p_{1}^{\text{com}})\epsilon_{\lambda_{2}}^{\nu}(p_{2}^{\text{com}})\epsilon_{\lambda_{3}}^{\rho*}(p_{3}^{\text{com}})\epsilon_{\lambda_{4}}^{\sigma*}(p_{4}^{\text{com}})M_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(p_{1}^{\text{com}},p_{2}^{\text{com}},p_{3}^{\text{com}},p_{4}^{\text{com}})~,

(272)

where the centre of mass frame momenta
p_{i}^{com}
were defined in (
104
) and the polarization vectors
\epsilon_{\lambda}^{\mu}(p)
were defined in (
187
). With this, we arrive at our result, which is as follows:

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(s,t,u)\big|_{s^{2}}

\displaystyle=2(4c_{1}+3c_{2})s^{2}~,

(273)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(s,t,u)\big|_{s^{2}}

\displaystyle=2(4c_{1}+c_{2})s^{2}~,

\displaystyle\Phi_{5}(s,t,u)\big|_{s^{2}}

\displaystyle=0~,

were we used the notation
\Phi_{i}(s,t,u)\big|_{s^{n}}
to emphasize that we only write the
\mathcal{O}(s^{n})
terms.
Comparing with (
6
), we deduce the following relations between the Wilson coefficients in the Lagrangian and the coefficients in the expansion of the amplitudes:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} g_{2}&=2(4c_{1}+3c_{2})~,\\
f_{2}&=2(4c_{1}+c_{2})~.\end{aligned}

(274)

O(
s^{3}
):

At this order, only the tree level amplitude of the dimension 10 Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{10}
contributes. Once again using FeynCalc, we arrive at

M_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=4c_{4}\,\eta_{\mu\nu}\eta_{\rho\sigma}s^{3}+\frac{1}{2}c_{3}\,{p_{4}}_{\mu}{p_{3}}_{\nu}\eta_{\rho\sigma}st+\ldots

(275)

Repeating the same steps as before,
i.e.
going to the COM frame and contracting with the polarization vectors, we compute the scattering amplitudes

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(s,t,u)\big|_{s^{3}}

\displaystyle=-4c_{4}s^{3}~,

(276)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(s,t,u)\big|_{s^{3}}

\displaystyle=-6(c_{3}+2c_{4}-c_{5})stu~,

\displaystyle\Phi_{5}(s,t,u)\big|_{s^{3}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{2}c_{3}stu~,

and upon comparing with (
6
), we see that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} g_{3}&=4c_{4}~,\\
f_{3}&=6(c_{3}+2c_{4}-c_{5})~,\\
h_{3}&=\frac{3}{2}c_{3}~.\end{aligned}

(277)

O(
s^{4}
):

We now have one-loop diagrams from the dimension 8 Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{8}
as well as tree level diagrams from the dimension 12 Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{12}
.
The one-loop diagrams are UV-divergent, using dimensional regularization (
d=4-2\epsilon
), they can be written in the following form:

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(s,t,u)\big|_{s^{4}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\xi_{1}}{\epsilon}+\xi_{0}\right)s^{4}+\left(\frac{\tilde{\xi}_{1}}{\epsilon}+\tilde{\xi}_{0}\right)s^{2}tu+s^{2}\left(\beta_{1,1}s^{2}+\beta_{1,2}tu\right)\log\left(-\frac{s}{\mu^{2}}\right)

(278)

\displaystyle+\beta_{1,3}s^{2}\left(t^{2}\log\left(-\frac{t}{\mu^{2}}\right)+u^{2}\log\left(-\frac{u}{\mu^{2}}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(s,t,u)\big|_{s^{4}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\epsilon}+\kappa_{0}\right)(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2})^{2}+\beta_{2}\left(s^{4}\log\left(-\frac{s}{\mu^{2}}\right)+t^{4}\log\left(-\frac{t}{\mu^{2}}\right)+u^{4}\log\left(-\frac{u}{\mu^{2}}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\Phi_{5}(s,t,u)\big|_{s^{4}}

\displaystyle=0~,

where
\mu^{2}
is the dimensional regularization scale
39
39

39

To be precise, this is actually the redefined scale
\mu^{2}\rightarrow\frac{e^{\gamma_{E}}}{4\pi}\mu^{2}
which is used to get rid of factors of
4\pi
and the Euler-Mascheroni constant
\gamma_{E}
.
and the various coefficients in the equation are given by

\displaystyle\xi_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{120\pi^{2}}\left(1008c_{1}^{2}+840c_{1}c_{2}+231c_{2}^{2}\right)~,

(279)

\displaystyle\xi_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1200\pi^{2}}\left(16912c_{1}^{2}+15000c_{1}c_{2}+4489c_{2}^{2}\right)~,

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{120\pi^{2}}\left(224c_{1}^{2}+304c_{1}c_{2}+110c_{2}^{2}\right)~,

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{1800\pi^{2}}\left(7664c_{1}^{2}+9304c_{1}c_{2}+2495c_{2}^{2}\right)~,

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{24\pi^{2}}\left(16c_{1}^{2}+16c_{1}c_{2}+3c_{2}^{2}\right)~,

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1440\pi^{2}}\left(6992c_{1}^{2}+8888c_{1}c_{2}+1773c_{2}^{2}\right)~,

\displaystyle\beta_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{120\pi}\left(912c_{1}^{2}+696c_{1}c_{2}+177c_{2}^{2}\right)~,

\displaystyle\beta_{1,2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{60\pi}\left(16c_{1}^{2}+8c_{1}c_{2}+c_{2}^{2}\right)~,

\displaystyle\beta_{1,3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{20\pi}\left(4c_{1}+3c_{2}\right)^{2}~,

\displaystyle\beta_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{12\pi^{2}}\left(16c_{1}^{2}+16c_{1}c_{2}+3c_{2}^{2}\right)~.

The UV divergences, which are now neatly captured by the
\frac{1}{\epsilon}
poles, can be cancelled by introducing dimension 12 counter-terms of the same form as in
\mathcal{L}_{12}
. The
\overline{MS}
regularization scheme corresponds to choosing the coefficients of the counter-terms such that they only cancel the divergences, without changing the finite parts. Explicitly, in this scheme we choose the following the counter-terms:

\displaystyle\delta c_{6}

\displaystyle=\frac{8\kappa_{1}}{\epsilon}~,

(280)

\displaystyle\delta c_{7}

\displaystyle=\frac{12\kappa_{1}+\xi_{1}}{\epsilon}~,

\displaystyle\delta c_{8}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\xi_{1}+2\tilde{\xi}_{1}}{\epsilon}~.

Having done this, and also including the contribution from tree level
\mathcal{L}_{12}
terms, we have the following result for the amplitude at order
s^{4}
in dimensional regularization with
\overline{MS}
scheme:

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(s,t,u)\big|_{s^{4}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{4}(2c_{7}-3c_{6})+\xi_{0}\right)s^{4}+\left(\frac{1}{2}(3c_{6}-2c_{7}-c_{8})+\tilde{\xi}_{0}\right)s^{2}tu

(281)

\displaystyle+s^{2}\left(\beta_{1,1}s^{2}+\beta_{1,2}tu\right)\log\left(-\frac{s}{\mu^{2}}\right)+\beta_{1,3}s^{2}\left(t^{2}\log\left(-\frac{t}{\mu^{2}}\right)+u^{2}\log\left(-\frac{u}{\mu^{2}}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(s,t,u)\big|_{s^{4}}

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{1}{8}c_{6}+\kappa_{0}\right)(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2})^{2}+\beta_{2}\left(s^{4}\log\left(-\frac{s}{\mu^{2}}\right)+t^{4}\log\left(-\frac{t}{\mu^{2}}\right)+u^{4}\log\left(-\frac{u}{\mu^{2}}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\Phi_{5}(s,t,u)\big|_{s^{4}}

\displaystyle=0~.

However, it is more convenient for our purposes to choose a different subtraction scheme. Firstly, we choose the dimensional regularization scale
\mu^{2}
to be related to the coefficient
g_{2}
:

\mu^{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{g_{2}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2(4c_{1}+3c_{2})}}.

(282)

We then choose the following subtraction scheme—the counter-terms also cancel out the finite pieces
\xi_{0}
,
\tilde{\xi}_{0}
and
\kappa_{0}
. Explicitly,

\displaystyle\delta c_{6}

\displaystyle=\frac{8\kappa_{1}}{\epsilon}+8\kappa_{0}~,

(283)

\displaystyle\delta c_{7}

\displaystyle=\frac{12\kappa_{1}+\xi_{1}}{\epsilon}+12\kappa_{0}+\xi_{0}~,

\displaystyle\delta c_{8}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\xi_{1}+2\tilde{\xi}_{1}}{\epsilon}+4\xi_{0}+2\tilde{\xi}_{0}~.

With this new scheme, we reach our final result:

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(s,t,u)\big|_{s^{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(2c_{7}-3c_{6})s^{4}+\frac{1}{2}(3c_{6}-2c_{7}-c_{8})s^{2}tu+s^{2}\left(\beta_{1,1}s^{2}+\beta_{1,2}tu\right)\log\left(-s\sqrt{g_{2}}\right)

(284)

\displaystyle+\beta_{1,3}s^{2}\left(t^{2}\log\left(-t\sqrt{g_{2}}\right)+u^{2}\log\left(-u\sqrt{g_{2}}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(s,t,u)\big|_{s^{4}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{8}c_{6}(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2})^{2}+\beta_{2}\left(s^{4}\log\left(-s\sqrt{g_{2}}\right)+t^{4}\log\left(-t\sqrt{g_{2}}\right)+u^{4}\log\left(-u\sqrt{g_{2}}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\Phi_{5}(s,t,u)\big|_{s^{4}}

\displaystyle=0~,

and comparing with (
6
) leads to the identification

\displaystyle g_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(2c_{7}-3c_{6})~,

(285)

\displaystyle g_{4}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(3c_{6}-2c_{7}-c_{8})~,

\displaystyle f_{4}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{8}c_{6}~.

As it is clear from the computation, the above relations are only valid for the chosen subtraction scheme, and a different choice of subtraction scheme, say for example
\overline{MS}
, would lead to terms involving
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
in the above equation.

Appendix E
Wilson coefficients in various models

In this appendix, we give some details on the amplitudes whose Wilson coefficients we use in section
4
. The corresponding EFTs are all obtained by integrating out massive particles which are weakly coupled at their production threshold. We consider resonances which can be integrated out at tree level (Yukawa-like theories) and charged particles which can be integrated out at one-loop (QED-like theories). Notice that these theories are not necessarily UV complete on their own: new particles might be necessary to restore unitarity above a larger energy scale.

E.1
Yukawa-like theories

This subsection collects a few examples where a low spin resonance is integrated out at tree level. We consider resonances of spin 0 and 2, even or odd under parity. The resulting Wilson coefficients are summarized in the first five rows of table
9
. Notice that a massive spin 1 resonance cannot couple to two photons. This is known as the Landau-Yang theorem
Landau:1948kw
;
Yang:1950rg
, and it simply follows from (
124
), (
125
). Indeed, a resonance of spin
\ell
only contributes to the partial wave of the same spin in the production channel, and there is no overlap of any two photon state with a spin 1 state.

There are multiple ways to derive the EFT. Since the field corresponding to the massive particle appears quadratically in the Lagrangian, one can evaluate the action on shell and expand at low energies, as done in
Henriksson:2021ymi
. Alternatively, one can compute the 2-to-2 tree level amplitude starting from the Feynman rules, or directly, using factorization and crossing, as explained in
Arkani-Hamed:2017jhn
. We will mostly follow the latter method, and we will comment on the relation to the Lagrangian construction only for the case of a spin 2 resonance.

We now illustrate the method, which will also serve as a lightning review. This subsection is not meant to be self-contained, see
Arkani-Hamed:2017jhn
for details. At tree level the on-shell pole due to exchange of a massive particle of spin
S
and mass
m
can be written as

\frac{M_{L,\;\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\{I_{1},I_{2}..I_{2S}\}}M_{R,\;\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4},\;\{I_{1},I_{2}\ldots I_{2S}\}}}{P^{2}+m^{2}}

(286)

where
M_{L}
and
M_{R}
are three-point amplitudes,
\lambda_{i}
are the photon helicities and
{I_{1},I_{2}\ldots I_{2S}}
are the little group indices of the massive particle. Consider now the construction of the two photon to one massive particle amplitude, with particle 3 being the massive one. This amplitude must be constructed out of the spinor-helicity variables
40
40

40

See appendix
B.2
for an introduction to spinor helicities for massless particles. In the massive case, the little group is
SO(3)
, and the index
I=1,\,2
is in the spin
1/2
representation,
i.e.
the fundamental of
su(2)
.

\xi_{1}^{\alpha},\tilde{\xi}_{1}^{\dot{\alpha}},\xi_{2}^{\alpha},\tilde{\xi}_{2}^{\dot{\alpha}},{\xi_{3}}^{I\,\alpha},{\tilde{\xi}_{3}}^{I\,\dot{\alpha}}

(287)

such that it has the right transformation property under the respective little groups of the three particles. For example the coupling of two positive helicity photons to spin 0 and spin 2 particles can be written as

\displaystyle\text{Spin 0}:\quad M_{++}^{\text{spin 0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{g}{m}[12]^{2},

(288)

\displaystyle\text{Spin 2}:\quad M_{++}^{\text{spin 2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{g}{m^{5}}\langle 1{\bf 3}\rangle\langle 2{\bf 3}\rangle[1{\bf 3}][2{\bf 3}][12]^{2},

where

[i\,{{\bf j}}]\equiv\xi_{i}^{\alpha}\xi_{j\,\alpha}^{I}\qquad\langle i\,{\bf j}\rangle\equiv\tilde{\xi}_{j\,\dot{\alpha}}\,\tilde{\xi}_{j}^{I\,\dot{\alpha}}\,.

(289)

We can now “glue” three-point couplings in (
286
) to deduce the four particle amplitude, keeping in mind that this prescription computes the all incoming amplitude. This procedure fixes the on shell residue of the amplitudes that we will present later in this section. Note that by choosing the coupling constants for
+
and
-
helicity photons appropriately, we can ensure that the pole lies in the parity even or odd channel. In principle one could have had other couplings, for example in the spin 2 case,

M_{++}^{\text{spin 2}}=\frac{g}{m^{7}}\langle 1{\bf 3}\rangle\langle 2{\bf 3}\rangle\langle 1{\bf 3}\rangle\langle 2{\bf 3}\rangle[12]^{4}.

(290)

However these are equivalent at the on shell mass pole i.e.
s=m^{2}
because

\langle 1{\bf 3}\rangle\equiv\tilde{\xi}_{1\,\dot{\alpha}}\,\tilde{\xi}_{3}^{I\dot{\alpha}}=-\frac{\tilde{\xi}_{1\,\dot{\alpha}}\,p_{3}^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\,\xi_{3\,\alpha}^{I}}{m}=\frac{\tilde{\xi}_{1\,\dot{\alpha}}\,p_{2}^{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\xi_{3\,\alpha}^{I}}{m}=\frac{\langle 12\rangle[2{\bf 3}]}{m},

(291)

and

\langle 12\rangle^{2}=[12]^{2}=s,

(292)

and as mentioned before, this procedure only fixes the on shell residue. Therefore we can trade powers of
s
for powers of
m^{2}
. Another way to understand this is that these couplings are the same up to contact terms. We choose the former coupling in (
288
) since it is consistent with low energy
s\rightarrow 0
and the high energy
s\rightarrow\infty
behaviour that we assume in this work. This point will be explained in more detail later in the appendix.

Scalar

A parity even neutral scalar particle of mass
m
can generically decay into two photons via the tree level coupling
\phi F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}
, where
\phi
is the field describing the particle. This interaction is not renormalizable, therefore the theory needs a UV completion. However, it is easy to imagine at least one: we can resolve the effective coupling by adding a charged particle (a scalar or a fermion) of mass
M>m
, with a Yukawa interaction with
\phi
. The resulting model is renormalizable. Here, we are interested in the EFT obtained by integrating out the charged particle. Its 2-to-2 photon amplitude at tree level can be easily constructed with the recipe given in
Arkani-Hamed:2017jhn
:

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}\frac{s^{2}}{s-m^{2}}~,

(293a)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}\left(\frac{s^{2}}{s-m^{2}}+\frac{t^{2}}{t-m^{2}}+\frac{u^{2}}{u-m^{2}}\right)~,

(293b)

\displaystyle\Phi_{5}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=0~,

(293c)

while
\Phi_{3}
and
\Phi_{4}
are obtained by crossing—see (
5
).
If, for instance, we complete the theory by coupling the scalar to a spin 1/2 charged fermion of mass
M
, the dimensionless coupling
\lambda
is proportional to the ratio
m/M
.
Regardless, the amplitude (
293
) shows that
m/\lambda
is the cutoff of the EFT. Indeed, when the Mandelstam invariants are of order
(m/\lambda)^{2}
, the amplitudes become large, the theory is strongly coupled and something new must happen to unitarize. On the other hand, for fixed
s
and
\lambda
small, the violations of unitarity contained in (
293
) can be cured by higher order terms in
\lambda
, as usual in effective field theory.

Axion

The case of a parity odd scalar proceeds much as in the previous example. This time the interaction vertex is
\tilde{\phi}F_{\mu\nu}\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}
,
\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}
being the Hodge dual of the field strength, and the amplitudes are

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}\frac{s^{2}}{s-m^{2}}~,

(294a)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}\left(\frac{s^{2}}{s-m^{2}}+\frac{t^{2}}{t-m^{2}}+\frac{u^{2}}{u-m^{2}}\right)~,

(294b)

\displaystyle\Phi_{5}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=0~.

(294c)

The only difference with the scalar case is a overall minus sign in the
\Phi_{2}
amplitude, which moves the production pole of the scalar resonance from the parity even to the parity odd partial wave.

Parity even spin 2

If we construct an amplitude for the tree level exchange of a spin 2 resonance following
Arkani-Hamed:2017jhn
, we find that there are multiple options. The ambiguity is parametrized by the three-point coupling between two photons and a massive spin 2 particle. A spin 2 massive particle can either decay into two photons with the same helicities, or into two photons with opposite helicities.
41
41

41

It is worth noticing that the spinor helicity structure for the decay into 2 photons with the same helicity is only unique once we ask for the absence of kinematic singularities,
i.e.
we demand that the
\Phi_{1}
amplitude has a low energy expansion of the kind (
6
).
The latter state has even parity—see (
123
)—hence it only exists for a parity even spin 2 resonance. On the other hand, a parity even resonance couples to the
(++)
and the
(--)
states equally, while a parity odd one couples to them with opposite sign. All in all, we have a two-parameters family of couplings of photons to a parity even spin 2 resonance. For simplicity, we only consider the limiting cases where the resonance does not couple to both the
(+-)
and the
++
state,
i.e.
we set the amplitude
\Phi_{5}
to zero. This is not necessary, but it is enough for our purposes. The two amplitudes are

\displaystyle\Phi^{\textup{I}}_{1}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{2}\frac{s^{2}}{m^{6}}\frac{t^{2}-4tu+u^{2}}{s-m^{2}}+\textup{polynomial}~,

(295a)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\textup{I}}_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{6}}\left(s^{2}\,\frac{t^{2}-4tu+u^{2}}{s-m^{2}}+t^{2}\,\frac{s^{2}-4su+u^{2}}{t-m^{2}}+u^{2}\,\frac{s^{2}-4st+t^{2}}{u-m^{2}}\right)+\textup{polynomial}~,

(295b)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\textup{I}}_{5}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=0~,

(295c)

for the coupling to equal helicity photons, and

\displaystyle\Phi^{\textup{II}}_{1}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}\left(\frac{s^{2}}{t-m^{2}}+\frac{s^{2}}{u-m^{2}}\right)+\textup{polynomial}~,

(296a)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\textup{II}}_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=0~,

(296b)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\textup{II}}_{5}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=0~,

(296c)

for the coupling to photons with opposite helicities. In (
295
) and (
296
), we allowed for yet unspecified polynomials in the Mandelstam invariants. Clearly, a polynomial does not change the residues of the amplitude. Therefore, these are ambiguities in the EFT of the resonance and the photons. Such degrees of freedom are parametrized by the Wilson coefficients as in (
6
), because the latter provide the most general polynomial solution to crossing without kinematic singularities. In other words, the polynomials correspond to contact interactions among the photons. We use this ambiguity to improve the Regge limit of the amplitudes (
295
) and (
296
). In particular, the type I amplitudes can be made compatible with the classical Regge growth conjecture
Chowdhury:2019kaq
,
i.e.
their growth can be limited to being
\mathcal{O}(s^{2},u^{2},t^{2})
, depending on which variable is taken large. This requires adding a homogeneous polynomial of degree 3. Furthermore, as explained in subsection
4.3
, we can add a homogeneous polynomial of degree 2 to further improve the Regge limit in the forward kinematics, so that both type I and type II amplitudes obey the dispersion relation (
37
). Notice that none of the additions modify the Wilson coefficients at dimension 12 (
g_{4},\,g_{4}^{\prime},\,f_{4}
). The final results are

\displaystyle\Phi^{\textup{I}}_{1}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{2}\frac{s^{2}}{m^{6}}\frac{t^{2}-4tu+u^{2}}{s-m^{2}}+~\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{6}}\left(m^{2}s^{2}+s^{3}\right),

(297a)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\textup{I}}_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{6}}\left(s^{2}\,\frac{t^{2}-4tu+u^{2}}{s-m^{2}}+t^{2}\,\frac{s^{2}-4su+u^{2}}{t-m^{2}}+u^{2}\,\frac{s^{2}-4st+t^{2}}{u-m^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{4}}\left(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2}\right)~,

(297b)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\textup{I}}_{5}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=0~,

(297c)

and

\displaystyle\Phi^{\textup{II}}_{1}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}\left(\frac{s^{2}}{t-m^{2}}+\frac{s^{2}}{u-m^{2}}\right)-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{4}}s^{2}~,

(298a)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\textup{II}}_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=0~,

(298b)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\textup{II}}_{5}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=0~.

(298c)

Let us now make contact with the work
Henriksson:2021ymi
. There, a single parity even coupling was considered, which in the present language is the type II coupling, without the addition of contact terms. The authors explicitly used a Lagrangian, and parametrized the coupling to the spin 2 resonance via

h^{\mu\nu}\left(\frac{u}{M}F_{\mu\rho}F_{\nu}{}^{\rho}+\frac{u^{\prime}}{M}\eta_{\mu\nu}F_{\rho\sigma}F^{\rho\sigma}\right)~,

(299)

where
h
is the field describing the resonance,
\eta
is the metric and
u,\,u^{\prime}
are coupling constants. Since the trace of
h
does not propagate, the
u^{\prime}
coupling does not produce poles, and, as pointed out in
Henriksson:2021ymi
, it is in fact equivalent to contact interactions, which can be fine tuned to produce an amplitude which does not grow more than
s^{2}
in the Regge limit. Since (
299
) is the most general parity even cubic coupling at lowest derivative order, our additional type I amplitudes must correspond to a higher derivative interaction. This is indeed the case, as it can be reversed engineered from the amplitude, following
Arkani-Hamed:2020blm
. The interaction producing equation (
295
) is

\frac{\lambda}{m^{3}}\left(\,h^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}F_{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\nu}F^{\rho\sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}h^{\mu\nu}F_{\rho\sigma}F^{\rho\sigma}\right)

(300)

As explained above, photon contact interactions can be added in order to obtain the Regge bounded amplitudes (
297
): if needed, their Lagrangian can be deduced combining (
297
) with (
14
) and (
17
).
It would be interesting to investigate the possible UV completions of the EFT of a spin 1 massless particle and a neutral spin 2 resonance, perhaps coupling them via a charged vector boson, and understand if the Lagrangian (
300
) can arise at low energies.

Parity odd spin 2

As mentioned in the previous paragraph, the lowest derivative order cubic coupling with a parity odd spin 2 particle involves two photons with equal helicities (type I). The corresponding amplitude, equipped with contact terms as above, is

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{2}\frac{s^{2}}{m^{6}}\frac{t^{2}-4tu+u^{2}}{s-m^{2}}+~\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{6}}\left(m^{2}s^{2}+s^{3}\right),

(301a)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{6}}\left(s^{2}\,\frac{t^{2}-4tu+u^{2}}{s-m^{2}}+t^{2}\,\frac{s^{2}-4su+u^{2}}{t-m^{2}}+u^{2}\,\frac{s^{2}-4st+t^{2}}{u-m^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{4}}\left(s^{2}+t^{2}+u^{2}\right)~,

(301b)

\displaystyle\Phi_{5}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=0~.

(301c)

All the same observations about the parity even type I amplitudes apply to this case as well. In particular, the Lagrangian which generates it is

\frac{\lambda}{m^{3}}\left(\,h^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}F_{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\nu}\tilde{F}^{\rho\sigma}-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}h^{\mu\nu}F_{\rho\sigma}\tilde{F}^{\rho\sigma}\right)

(302)

E.2
QED-like theories

Let us briefly consider scalar, spinor and vector QED.
By integrating out the massive charged particle (i.e. respectively the fermion, scalar and vector), one obtains the Wilson coefficients of the photon Lagrangian (
13
). The case of standard QED leads to the well known Euler-Heisenberg Lagrangian
Heisenberg:1936nmg
.

Here, we comment on the Regge limit of the one-loop amplitudes, from which the value in table
9
are easily obtained.
In all three cases, the amplitudes can be explicitly written in a basis of three integrals
berestetskii2012quantum
;
Yang:1994nu
;
PhysRevD.52.5018
.
These integrals can be estimated when the Mandelstam invariants become large, and one can conclude that these one-loop amplitudes grow in the Regge limit at most as
\mathcal{O}((\log s)^{2})
.

Finally, note that these QED theories are not UV complete on their own (see appendix
F
) and should be considered as partial UV completion of the photon EFT.

Appendix F
UV incompleteness of QED

This appendix is a short summary of the results derived in
Peskin:1995ev
for quantum electrodynamics (QED).

Let us consider the Lagrangian density of QED, it reads

\mathcal{L}_{QED}=\bar{\psi}(i\not{D}-m)\psi-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2\xi}(\partial_{\mu}A^{\mu})^{2}+\text{counter terms}.

(303)

Here
\psi(x)
is the Dirac fermion field with the physical mass
m
and the covariant derivative is

D_{\mu}\equiv\partial_{\mu}+ieA_{\mu},

(304)

where
e
is the electric charge of
\psi
.
The excitations of
\psi(x)
describe electrons and positrons with mass
m
.
The electromagnetic field strength tensor
F_{\mu\nu}(x)
is given by

F_{\mu\nu}(x)\equiv\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}(x)-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}(x),

(305)

where
A_{\mu}(x)
is the electromagnetic potential. The excitations of
A_{\mu}(x)
describe photons. One usually also defines the electromagnetic constant
\alpha
as

\alpha\equiv e^{2}/4\pi.

(306)

Notice that the counter terms in (
303
) are adjusted in such a way that
m
and
\alpha
are physical finite constants. QED describes interaction between electrons, positrons and light in our world. The experimentally measured value of
\alpha
is

\alpha\approx\frac{1}{137.035}.

(307)

Let us discuss what observables can be computed in QED and how the result (
307
) can be measured experimentally.

The electromagnetic form factor is defined as the following matrix element

{}_{\text{out}}^{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}\langle m,\vec{p}_{1};m,\vec{p}_{2}|J^{\mu}(x=0)|0\rangle,

(308)

where
J^{\mu}(x)
is the electromagnetic current and in the left-hand side we have a two-particle “out” asymptotic state built out of an electron and positron with helicities
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
and 3-momenta
\vec{p}_{1}
and
\vec{p}_{2}
.
Due to the Lorentz invariance this form factor can be decomposed into tensor structures as

{}_{\text{out}}^{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}\langle m,\vec{p}_{1};m,\vec{p}_{2}|J^{\mu}(x=0)|0\rangle=F_{1}(q^{2})\times(\bar{u}_{\lambda_{1}}\gamma^{\mu}u_{\lambda_{2}})+F_{2}(q^{2})\times\frac{iq_{\nu}}{2m}\,(\bar{u}_{\lambda_{1}}\sigma^{\mu\nu}u_{\lambda_{2}}),

(309)

where
u_{\lambda}
is the 4-component solution of the Dirac equation.
Here
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are the scalar components of the electromagnetic form factor. The total momentum
q
of the two-particle state is

q^{\mu}\equiv p_{1}^{\mu}+p_{2}^{\mu}.

(310)

For a generic discussion of form factors see
Karateev:2019ymz
;
Karateev:2020axc
.

The functions
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are computed to one-loop in section 6 in
Peskin:1995ev
, see equations (6.56) and (6.57). Using the (semi-classical) Born approximation one can compute the expression for the magnetic moment of the electron in terms of the Landé g-factor which reads as

g=2+2F_{2}(0),\qquad F_{2}(0)=\frac{\alpha}{2\pi}.

(311)

By measuring
g
one can determine
\alpha
via the above relation.

The effective Coulomb potential was discussed in subsection 7.5 in
Peskin:1995ev
. One finds that

V(r)=-\frac{f(r)}{r},

(312)

where the function
f(r)
computed in QED to one loop has the form

f(r)=\alpha+\alpha^{2}\,h(r)+O(\alpha^{3}).

(313)

The function
f(r)
is often called the running coupling constant. The function
h(r)
can be evaluated analytically at large and small distances. One gets

h(r)\underset{mr\gg 1}{=}\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{e^{-2mr}}{(mr)^{3/2}},\qquad h(r)\underset{mr\ll 1}{=}-\frac{1}{54\pi}(5/6+\gamma+\log(mr)),

(314)

where
\gamma
is the Euler gamma. Looking at (
313
) and (
314
) one finds the following asymptotic behavior

f(\infty)=\alpha,\qquad f(0)=\infty.

(315)

At small distance or equivalently at high energies the function
f(r)
blows up. This indicates that the theory is not well-defined at arbitrary high energies.
In fact, one expects the coupling to diverge at a finite energy scale.
This problem is known as the Landau pole. The Landau pole issue was discovered perturbatively but is believed to hold non-perturbatively.

Appendix G
LSZ derivation of crossing equations

We begin in subsection
G.1
with a derivation of crossing in general frame using the LSZ prescription, as defined in many textbooks, with the gauge fixed correlator of the
A_{\mu}
fields. However, we find this prescription not as satisfactory as working with the gauge invariant correlator of field strengths
F_{\mu\nu}
. Therefore, in subsection
G.2
we will use the latter to re-derive crossing in both general and COM frame.

G.1
Gauge fixed LSZ prescription

The LSZ reduction formula for the scattering process
12\rightarrow 34
of four massless spin-1 particles can be written in the following form
Srednicki:2007qs

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{4}x_{1}\,d^{4}x_{2}\,d^{4}x_{3}\,d^{4}x_{4}

(316)

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad e^{-ip_{3}x_{3}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{3}*}_{\lambda_{3}}(p_{3})(-\partial^{2}_{3})

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad e^{-ip_{4}x_{4}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{4}*}_{\lambda_{4}}(p_{4})(-\partial^{2}_{4})

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad\langle\Omega|T\{A_{\mu_{4}}(x_{4})A_{\mu_{3}}(x_{3})A_{\mu_{1}}(x_{1})A_{\mu_{2}}(x_{2})\}|\Omega\rangle_{connected}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{1}^{2}})\epsilon^{\mu_{1}}_{\lambda_{1}}(p_{1})\,e^{ip_{1}x_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{2}^{2}})\epsilon^{\mu_{2}}_{\lambda_{2}}(p_{2})\,e^{ip_{2}x_{2}},

where
|\Omega\rangle
denotes the vacuum state and
A_{\mu_{i}}(x)
are massless spin 1 fields in the Lorentz gauge. Similarly we can write the LSZ reduction formula for the process
1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}^{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{3},p_{2},p_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{4}x_{1}\,d^{4}x_{2}\,d^{4}x_{3}\,d^{4}x_{4}

(317)

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad e^{-ip_{2}x_{2}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{2}*}_{\lambda_{2}}(p_{2})(-\partial^{2}_{2})

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad e^{-ip_{4}x_{4}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{4}*}_{\lambda_{4}}(p_{4})(-\partial^{2}_{4})

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad\langle\Omega|T\{A_{\mu_{4}}(x_{4})A_{\mu_{2}}(x_{2})A_{\mu_{1}}(x_{1})A_{\mu_{3}}(x_{3})\}|\Omega\rangle_{connected}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial^{2}_{1}})\epsilon^{\mu_{1}}_{\lambda_{1}}(p_{1})\,e^{ip_{1}x_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial^{2}_{3}})\epsilon^{\mu_{3}}_{\lambda_{3}}(p_{3})\,e^{ip_{3}x_{3}},

Note that the amplitudes above are defined for positive energy momenta, namely
p_{i}^{0}>0
. Crossing symmetry is the statement that the amplitudes for the two processes above are related by analytic continuation. Consider the
1\bar{3}\rightarrow 24
process and analytically continue the expression
317
in
p_{2}
and
p_{3}
to allow for negative energies.

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}^{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},-p_{3},-p_{2},p_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{4}x_{1}\,d^{4}x_{2}\,d^{4}x_{3}\,d^{4}x_{4}

(318)

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad e^{ip_{2}x_{2}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{2}*}_{\lambda_{2}}(-p_{2})(-\partial^{2}_{2})

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad e^{-ip_{4}x_{4}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{4}*}_{\lambda_{4}}(p_{4})(-\partial^{2}_{4})

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad\langle\Omega|T\{A_{\mu_{4}}(x_{4})A_{\mu_{2}}(x_{2})A_{\mu_{1}}(x_{1})A_{\mu_{3}}(x_{3})\}|\Omega\rangle_{connected}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial^{2}_{1}})\epsilon^{\mu_{1}}_{\lambda_{1}}(p_{1})\,e^{ip_{1}x_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial^{2}_{3}})\epsilon^{\mu_{3}}_{\lambda_{3}}(-p_{3})\,e^{-ip_{3}x_{3}},

We see that the above expression looks very similar to (
316
) except for the correlator and the polarization vectors which are evaluated at negative energies.
Using the fact that bosonic operators commute we see that the correlation functions are equal

\langle\Omega|T\{A_{\mu_{4}}(x_{4})A_{\mu_{3}}(x_{3})A_{\mu_{1}}(x_{1})A_{\mu_{2}}(x_{2})\}|\Omega\rangle=\\
\langle\Omega|T\{A_{\mu_{4}}(x_{4})A_{\mu_{2}}(x_{2})A_{\mu_{1}}(x_{1})A_{\mu_{3}}(x_{3})\}|\Omega\rangle

(319)

We now consider the negative energy polarization vectors
\epsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda}(-p)
. They are the analytic continuations of the positive energy polarizations, which were defined in
187
. Since we deal with massless particles in this work, we choose the following analytic continuation for the momenta

p^{0}\rightarrow-p^{0}\quad,\boldsymbol{p}\rightarrow-\boldsymbol{p},\quad\theta\rightarrow\theta,\quad\phi\rightarrow\phi

(320)

which ensures that
p^{\mu}\rightarrow-p^{\mu}
. See
Hebbar:2020ukp
for more details. Under this analytic continuation we see from the explicit form
187
that

\epsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda}(-p)=\epsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda}(p)=\epsilon^{\,\mu*}_{-\lambda}(p)

(321)

Thus by using
321
and
319
in
318
and then comparing with
316
we have

\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{}_{+\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{3}}^{-\lambda_{2},+\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},-p_{3},-p_{2},p_{4}).

(322)

Analogously one derives the other three crossing equations. The complete summary of crossing equations reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{}_{-\lambda_{4},+\lambda_{2}}^{+\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{1}}(-p_{4},p_{2},p_{3},-p_{1}),

(323)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{}_{+\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{3}}^{-\lambda_{2},+\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},-p_{3},-p_{2},p_{4}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}_{\bar{3}2\rightarrow\bar{1}4}{}_{-\lambda_{3},+\lambda_{2}}^{-\lambda_{1},+\lambda_{4}}(-p_{3},p_{2},-p_{1},p_{4}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}_{1\bar{4}\rightarrow 3\bar{2}}{}_{+\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{4}}^{+\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{2}}(p_{1},-p_{4},p_{3},-p_{2}).

Specializing to the case of scattering of identical neutral massless spin 1 particles, all the processes in the above 4 equations are the same and thus the crossing equations express a symmetry of the scattering amplitude.

All incoming amplitude

Using the LSZ reduction formula
316
it is possible to define an unphysical 4 photons to nothing amplitude by analytic continuation:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\,\lambda_{1},\,\lambda_{2}}^{-\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},-p_{3},-p_{4})

(325)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{4}x_{1}\,d^{4}x_{2}\,d^{4}x_{3}\,d^{4}x_{4}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad e^{ip_{3}x_{3}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{3}}_{\lambda_{3}}(p_{3})(-\partial^{2}_{3})

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad e^{ip_{4}x_{4}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{4}}_{\lambda_{4}}(p_{4})(-\partial^{2}_{4})

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad\langle\Omega|T\{A_{\mu_{4}}(x_{4})A_{\mu_{3}}(x_{3})A_{\mu_{1}}(x_{1})A_{\mu_{2}}(x_{2})\}|\Omega\rangle_{connected}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{1}^{2}})\epsilon^{\mu_{1}}_{\lambda_{1}}(p_{1})\,e^{ip_{1}x_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{2}^{2}})\epsilon^{\mu_{2}}_{\lambda_{2}}(p_{2})\,e^{ip_{2}x_{2}},

where we used
321
. The benefit of defining this unphysical amplitude is that it is manifestly
S_{4}
permutation invariant.

G.2
Gauge invariant LSZ prescription

We begin by considering the residue at the on-shell pole of a 4 point correlator of electromagnetic tensor
F_{\mu\nu}
, see for example Eq.10.3.2 of
Weinberg:1995mt
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}H_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}^{\lambda_{1}\,}(p_{1})

\displaystyle H_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}^{\lambda_{2}\,}(p_{2})H_{\lambda_{3},\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}(p_{3})H_{\lambda_{4},\mu_{4}\nu_{4}}(p_{4})\;\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

(326)

\displaystyle=\int d^{4}x_{1}\,d^{4}x_{2}\,d^{4}x_{3}\,d^{4}x_{4}\,e^{-ip_{3}x_{3}}\,e^{-ip_{4}x_{4}}\,(-\partial^{2}_{3})\,(-\partial^{2}_{4})

\displaystyle\times\,\langle\Omega|T\{F_{\mu_{4}\nu_{4}}(x_{4})F_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}(x_{3})F_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(x_{1})F_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}(x_{2})\}|\Omega\rangle_{connected}

\displaystyle(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{1}^{2}})(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{2}^{2}})\,e^{ip_{1}x_{1}}\,e^{ip_{2}x_{2}}

where the object
H_{\lambda,\mu\nu}
is the same as (
184
) because in free theory we have

H_{\lambda,\mu\nu}(p)=\langle 0|F_{\mu\nu}(0)|p,\lambda\rangle\,.

(327)

Unfortunately it is not easy to invert this object in a covariant way to extract the scattering amplitude. Instead we use the following relation which follows from the orthogonality and transversality of photon polarization vectors:

\epsilon^{\mu\,*}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(p)H_{\lambda,\mu\nu}(p)=p_{\nu}\delta_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}

(328)

to arrive at

\displaystyle{p_{1}}_{\nu_{1}}{p_{2}}_{\nu_{2}}{p_{3}}_{\nu_{3}}{p_{4}}_{\nu_{4}}\;\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}

\displaystyle(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

(329)

\displaystyle=\int d^{4}x_{1}\,d^{4}x_{2}\,d^{4}x_{3}\,d^{4}x_{4}

\displaystyle\times\quad e^{-ip_{3}x_{3}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{3}*}_{\lambda_{3}}(p_{3})(-\partial^{2}_{3})

\displaystyle\times\quad e^{-ip_{4}x_{4}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{4}*}_{\lambda_{4}}(p_{4})(-\partial^{2}_{4})

\displaystyle\times\quad\,\langle\Omega|T\{F_{\mu_{4}\nu_{4}}(x_{4})F_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}(x_{3})F_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(x_{1})F_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}(x_{2})\}|\Omega\rangle_{connected}

\displaystyle\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{1}^{2}})\epsilon^{\mu_{1}}_{\lambda_{1}}(p_{1})\,e^{ip_{1}x_{1}}

\displaystyle\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{2}^{2}})\epsilon^{\mu_{2}}_{\lambda_{2}}(p_{2})\,e^{ip_{2}x_{2}},

At this stage, we can evaluate the above equation in the COM frame (
104
) and extract the amplitude as follows:

\displaystyle\;\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\int d^{4}x_{1}\,d^{4}x_{2}\,d^{4}x_{3}\,d^{4}x_{4}

(330)

\displaystyle\times\quad e^{-ip_{3}^{\text{com}}x_{3}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{3}*}_{\lambda_{3}}(p_{3}^{\text{com}})\mathfrak{v}^{\nu_{3}}(-\partial^{2}_{3})

\displaystyle\times\quad e^{-ip_{4}^{\text{com}}x_{4}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{4}*}_{\lambda_{4}}(p_{4}^{\text{com}})\mathfrak{v}^{\nu_{4}}(-\partial^{2}_{4})

\displaystyle\times\quad\,\langle\Omega|T\{F_{\mu_{4}\nu_{4}}(x_{4})F_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}(x_{3})F_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(x_{1})F_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}(x_{2})\}|\Omega\rangle_{connected}

\displaystyle\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{1}^{2}})\epsilon^{\mu_{1}}_{\lambda_{1}}(p_{1}^{\text{com}})\mathfrak{v}^{\nu_{1}}\,e^{ip_{1}^{\text{com}}x_{1}}

\displaystyle\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{2}^{2}})\epsilon^{\mu_{2}}_{\lambda_{2}}(p_{2}^{\text{com}})\mathfrak{v}^{\nu_{2}}\,e^{ip_{2}^{\text{com}}x_{2}},

where the vector
\mathfrak{v}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{s}}(-1,0,0,0)
is chosen so that

\mathfrak{v}^{\mu}{p_{i}}^{\text{com}}_{\mu}=1\quad\forall\quad i=1,2,3,4

(331)

Crossing using LSZ

Consider the LSZ formula for the process
13\rightarrow 24

\displaystyle{p_{1}}_{\nu_{1}}{p_{3}}_{\nu_{3}}{p_{2}}_{\nu_{2}}{p_{4}}_{\nu_{4}}\;\mathcal{T}_{13\rightarrow 24}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}^{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{4}}

\displaystyle(p_{1},p_{3},p_{2},p_{4})

(332)

\displaystyle=\int d^{4}x_{1}\,d^{4}x_{2}\,d^{4}x_{3}\,d^{4}x_{4}

\displaystyle\times\quad e^{-ip_{2}x_{2}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{2}*}_{\lambda_{2}}(p_{2})(-\partial^{2}_{2})

\displaystyle\times\quad e^{-ip_{4}x_{4}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{4}*}_{\lambda_{4}}(p_{4})(-\partial^{2}_{4})

\displaystyle\times\quad\,\langle\Omega|T\{F_{\mu_{4}\nu_{4}}(x_{4})F_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}(x_{2})F_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(x_{1})F_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}(x_{3})\}|\Omega\rangle_{connected}

\displaystyle\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{1}^{2}})\epsilon^{\mu_{1}}_{\lambda_{1}}(p_{1})\,e^{ip_{1}x_{1}}

\displaystyle\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{3}^{2}})\epsilon^{\mu_{3}}_{\lambda_{3}}(p_{3})\,e^{ip_{3}x_{3}}.

A priori this formula and (
329
) are both defined for positive energy momenta
p_{i}^{0}\geq 0
. But suppose we can analytically continue the
formulae to negative energy momenta as well, then evaluating (
332
) at
-p_{2}
and
-p_{3}
gives

\displaystyle{p_{1}}_{\nu_{1}}{p_{3}}_{\nu_{3}}{p_{2}}_{\nu_{2}}{p_{4}}_{\nu_{4}}\;\mathcal{T}_{13\rightarrow 24}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}^{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{4}}

\displaystyle(p_{1},-p_{3},-p_{2},p_{4})

(333)

\displaystyle=\int d^{4}x_{1}\,d^{4}x_{2}\,d^{4}x_{3}\,d^{4}x_{4}

\displaystyle\times\quad e^{ip_{2}x_{2}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{2}*}_{\lambda_{2}}(-p_{2})(-\partial^{2}_{2})

\displaystyle\times\quad e^{-ip_{4}x_{4}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{4}*}_{\lambda_{4}}(p_{4})(-\partial^{2}_{4})

\displaystyle\times\quad\,\langle\Omega|T\{F_{\mu_{4}\nu_{4}}(x_{4})F_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}(x_{2})F_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(x_{1})F_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}(x_{3})\}|\Omega\rangle_{connected}

\displaystyle\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{1}^{2}})\epsilon^{\mu_{1}}_{\lambda_{1}}(p_{1})\,e^{ip_{1}x_{1}}

\displaystyle\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{3}^{2}})\epsilon^{\mu_{3}}_{\lambda_{3}}(-p_{3})\,e^{-ip_{3}x_{3}},

which looks very similar to (
329
), except for the correlator and the polarization vectors which are evaluated at negative energies.
Using the fact that bosonic operators commute we see that the correlation functions are equal

\langle\Omega|T\{F_{\mu_{4}\nu_{4}}(x_{4})F_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}(x_{3})F_{\mu_{1}\mu_{1}}(x_{1})F_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}(x_{2})\}|\Omega\rangle=\\
\langle\Omega|T\{F_{\mu_{4}\nu_{4}}(x_{4})F_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}(x_{2})F_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(x_{1})F_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}(x_{3})\}|\Omega\rangle

(334)

We now consider the negative energy polarization vectors
\epsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda}(-p)
. They are the analytic continuations of the positive energy polarizations, which were defined in
187
. Since we deal with massless particles in this work, we choose the following analytic continuation for the momenta

p^{0}\rightarrow-p^{0}\quad,\boldsymbol{p}\rightarrow-\boldsymbol{p},\quad\theta\rightarrow\theta,\quad\phi\rightarrow\phi

(335)

which ensures that
p^{\mu}\rightarrow-p^{\mu}
. See
Hebbar:2020ukp
for more details. Under this analytic continuation we see from the explicit form
187
that

\epsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda}(-p)=\epsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda}(p)=\left(\epsilon^{\,\mu}_{-\lambda}(p)\right)^{*}

(336)

Thus we have

\displaystyle{p_{1}}_{\nu_{1}}{p_{2}}_{\nu_{2}}{p_{3}}_{\nu_{3}}{p_{4}}_{\nu_{4}}\;\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}

\displaystyle(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

(337)

\displaystyle={p_{1}}_{\nu_{1}}{p_{3}}_{\nu_{3}}{p_{2}}_{\nu_{2}}{p_{4}}_{\nu_{4}}\;\mathcal{T}_{13\rightarrow 24}{}_{\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{3}}^{-\lambda_{2},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},-p_{3},-p_{2},p_{4})\,,

which implies the crossing equation in any frame

\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\mathcal{T}_{13\rightarrow 24}{}_{\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{3}}^{-\lambda_{2},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},-p_{3},-p_{2},p_{4})\,.

(338)

Crossing for COM frame amplitudes

Our prescription to arrive at the amplitude involved going to a special frame, the COM frame. But this presents a problem - it is not possible to have both the processes in their COM frame in the above relation (
338
). For example putting say the s channel amplitude on the right hand side in its COM frame would mean that the
t
-channel amplitude on the left hand side is in a frame different from its COM frame (which we called crossed frame). The way to deal with this, as was reviewed in
Hebbar:2020ukp
, is to take a step back and first perform a Lorentz transformation and then analytically continue the amplitude,
i.e.
cross the amplitude.
More concretely, consider (
329
) in the (
s
-channel) COM frame and perform the following (complexified) Lorentz transformation

\Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{i\sqrt{-u}}{\sqrt{s}}&0&-\frac{i\sqrt{-t}}{\sqrt{s}}\\
-\frac{\sqrt{-u}}{\sqrt{-t}}&1&0&\frac{\sqrt{-u}}{\sqrt{-t}}\\
0&0&1&0\\
-\frac{i\sqrt{s}}{\sqrt{-t}}&\frac{i\sqrt{-u}}{\sqrt{s}}&0&-\frac{iu}{\sqrt{s}\sqrt{-t}}\end{pmatrix}

(339)

such that

\displaystyle p_{1}^{\text{com}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2}(1,0,0,1)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{1}\equiv\Lambda p_{1}=-\frac{i\sqrt{-t}}{2}(1,0,0,1)

(340)

\displaystyle p_{2}^{\text{com}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2}(1,0,0,-1)\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{2}\equiv\Lambda p_{2}=-\frac{i\sqrt{-t}}{2}\left(-1,\frac{2i\sqrt{-su}}{t},0,\frac{u-s}{t}\right)

\displaystyle p_{3}^{\text{com}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2}\left(1,\frac{2\sqrt{tu}}{s},0,\frac{t-u}{s}\right)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{3}\equiv\Lambda p_{3}=-\frac{i\sqrt{-t}}{2}(-1,0,0,1)

\displaystyle p_{4}^{\text{com}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2}\left(1,-\frac{2\sqrt{tu}}{s},0,\frac{u-t}{s}\right)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{4}\equiv\Lambda p_{4}=-\frac{i\sqrt{-t}}{2}\left(1,\frac{2i\sqrt{-su}}{t},0,\frac{u-s}{t}\right)

Note that we are still in the
s
-channel region
s>0
and
t<0
.
For this choice of Lorentz transformation
\Lambda
, the photon polarization vectors also transform in the same way
\epsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda}(\tilde{p})=\epsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda}(\Lambda p)={\Lambda^{\mu}}_{\nu}\epsilon^{\nu}_{\lambda}(p)

42
42

42

In general we have
\epsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda}(\Lambda p)=e^{-i\lambda\omega}{\Lambda^{\mu}}_{\nu}\epsilon^{\nu}_{\lambda}(p)
. For a Lorentz transformation composed of boosts and rotations in the
x-z
plane, the Wigner angle
\omega
is
0
. See equation A.112 in
Hebbar:2020ukp
.
(upto terms proportional to the momentum)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}(p_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,1,i\lambda,0)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}(\tilde{p}_{1})=\Lambda\epsilon_{\lambda}(p_{1})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,1,i\lambda,0)

(341)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}(p_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,-1,i\lambda,0)\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}(\tilde{p}_{2})=\Lambda\epsilon_{\lambda}(p_{2})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,\frac{s-u}{t},i\lambda,\frac{2i\sqrt{-su}}{t})

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}(p_{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,\frac{t-u}{s},i\lambda,-\frac{2\sqrt{tu}}{s})

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}(\tilde{p}_{3})=\Lambda\epsilon_{\lambda}(p_{3})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,-1,i\lambda,0)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}(p_{4})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,\frac{u-t}{s},i\lambda,\frac{2\sqrt{tu}}{s})

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}(\tilde{p}_{4})=\Lambda\epsilon_{\lambda}(p_{4})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,\frac{u-s}{t},i\lambda,-\frac{2i\sqrt{-su}}{t})

Under this Lorentz transformation, the amplitude transforms as (
177
)
43
43

43

The little group phases
t_{i}=1
since the Wigner angle is
0
. See footnote
42
above.

\displaystyle{\tilde{p_{1}}}_{\nu_{1}}{\tilde{p_{2}}}_{\nu_{2}}{\tilde{p_{3}}}_{\nu_{3}}{\tilde{p_{4}}}_{\nu_{4}}\;\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)={\tilde{p_{1}}}_{\nu_{1}}{\tilde{p_{2}}}_{\nu_{2}}{\tilde{p_{3}}}_{\nu_{3}}{\tilde{p_{4}}}_{\nu_{4}}\;\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2},\tilde{p}_{3},\tilde{p}_{4})

(342)

Writing out the right hand side

\displaystyle{\tilde{p_{1}}}_{\nu_{1}}{\tilde{p_{2}}}_{\nu_{2}}{\tilde{p_{3}}}_{\nu_{3}}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\tilde{p_{4}}}_{\nu_{4}}\;\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

(343)

\displaystyle=\int d^{4}x_{1}\,d^{4}x_{2}\,d^{4}x_{3}\,d^{4}x_{4}

\displaystyle\times\quad e^{-i\tilde{p}_{3}x_{3}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{3}*}_{\lambda_{3}}(\tilde{p}_{3})(-\partial^{2}_{3})

\displaystyle\times\quad e^{-i\tilde{p}_{4}x_{4}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{4}*}_{\lambda_{4}}(\tilde{p}_{4})(-\partial^{2}_{4})

\displaystyle\times\quad\,\langle\Omega|T\{F_{\mu_{4}\nu_{4}}(x_{4})F_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}(x_{3})F_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(x_{1})F_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}(x_{2})\}|\Omega\rangle_{connected}

\displaystyle\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{1}^{2}})\epsilon^{\mu_{1}}_{\lambda_{1}}(\tilde{p}_{1})\,e^{i\tilde{p}_{1}x_{1}}

\displaystyle\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{2}^{2}})\epsilon^{\mu_{2}}_{\lambda_{2}}(\tilde{p}_{2})\,e^{i\tilde{p}_{2}x_{2}},

we now analytically continue to the
t
-channel region
t>0
and
s<0
at fixed real
u
. Under this operation we have

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i\sqrt{-t}}{2}(1,0,0,1)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{1}^{\text{a.c.}}=\frac{\sqrt{t}}{2}(1,0,0,1)

(344)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i\sqrt{-t}}{2}\left(-1,\frac{2i\sqrt{-su}}{t},0,\frac{u-s}{t}\right)\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{2}^{\text{a.c.}}=-\frac{\sqrt{t}}{2}\left(1,\frac{2\sqrt{su}}{t},0,\frac{t-u}{s}\right)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{3}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i\sqrt{-t}}{2}(-1,0,0,1)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{3}^{\text{a.c.}}=-\frac{\sqrt{t}}{2}(1,0,0,-1)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{4}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i\sqrt{-t}}{2}\left(1,\frac{2i\sqrt{-su}}{t},0,\frac{u-s}{t}\right)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{4}^{\text{a.c.}}=\frac{\sqrt{t}}{2}\left(1,-\frac{2\sqrt{su}}{t},0,\frac{u-s}{t}\right)

which ensures that the momenta
P_{1}^{\overline{com}}=\tilde{p}_{1}^{\text{a.c.}}
,
P_{3}^{\overline{com}}=-\tilde{p}_{3}^{\text{a.c.}}
,
P_{2}^{\overline{com}}=-\tilde{p}_{2}^{\text{a.c.}}
and
P_{4}^{\overline{com}}=\tilde{p}_{4}^{\text{a.c.}}
are precisely in the
t
-channel COM frame. We also analytically continue the photon polarizations

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}(\tilde{p}_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,1,i\lambda,0)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}^{\text{a.c.}}(\tilde{p}_{1})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,1,i\lambda,0)

(345)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}(\tilde{p}_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,\frac{s-u}{t},i\lambda,\frac{2i\sqrt{-su}}{t})\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}^{\text{a.c.}}(\tilde{p}_{2})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,\frac{s-u}{t},i\lambda,-\frac{2\sqrt{su}}{t})

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}(\tilde{p}_{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,-1,i\lambda,0)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}^{\text{a.c.}}(\tilde{p}_{3})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,-1,i\lambda,0)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}(\tilde{p}_{4})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,\frac{u-s}{t},i\lambda,-\frac{2i\sqrt{-su}}{t})

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}^{\text{a.c.}}(\tilde{p}_{4})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(0,\frac{u-s}{t},i\lambda,\frac{2\sqrt{su}}{t})\,.

Using
\epsilon^{\mu}_{\lambda}(-p)=\left(\epsilon^{\mu}_{-\lambda}(p)\right)^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle{\tilde{p_{1}}}_{\nu_{1}}{\tilde{p_{2}}}_{\nu_{2}}{\tilde{p_{3}}}_{\nu_{3}}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\tilde{p_{4}}}_{\nu_{4}}\;\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

(346)

\displaystyle=\int d^{4}x_{1}\,d^{4}x_{2}\,d^{4}x_{3}\,d^{4}x_{4}

\displaystyle\times\quad e^{iP_{3}^{\overline{com}}x_{3}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{3}*}_{-\lambda_{3}}(P_{3}^{\overline{com}})(-\partial^{2}_{3})

\displaystyle\times\quad e^{-iP_{4}^{\overline{com}}x_{4}}\,\epsilon^{\mu_{4}*}_{\lambda_{4}}(P_{4}^{\overline{com}})(-\partial^{2}_{4})

\displaystyle\times\quad\,\langle\Omega|T\{F_{\mu_{4}\nu_{4}}(x_{4})F_{\mu_{3}\nu_{3}}(x_{3})F_{\mu_{1}\nu_{1}}(x_{1})F_{\mu_{2}\nu_{2}}(x_{2})\}|\Omega\rangle_{connected}

\displaystyle\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{1}^{2}})\epsilon^{\mu_{1}}_{\lambda_{1}}(P_{1}^{\overline{com}})\,e^{iP_{1}^{\overline{com}}x_{1}}

\displaystyle\times\quad(-\overleftarrow{\partial_{2}^{2}})\epsilon^{\mu_{2}}_{-\lambda_{2}}(P_{2}^{\overline{com}})\,e^{-iP_{2}^{\overline{com}}x_{2}},

the right hand side is nothing but the t channel amplitude evaluated in its COM frame with the values for its arguments given by

\displaystyle\bar{s}=-(P_{1}^{\overline{com}}+P_{3}^{\overline{com}})^{2}

\displaystyle=-(\tilde{p}_{1}-\tilde{p}_{3})^{2}=t

(347)

\displaystyle\bar{t}=-(P_{1}^{\overline{com}}-P_{2}^{\overline{com}})^{2}

\displaystyle=-(\tilde{p}_{1}+\tilde{p}_{2})^{2}=s\,,

and therefore we deduce that

{P_{1}}^{\overline{com}}_{\nu_{1}}{P_{2}}^{\overline{com}}_{\nu_{2}}{P_{3}}^{\overline{com}}_{\nu_{3}}{P_{4}}^{\overline{com}}_{\nu_{4}}\;\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)={P_{1}}^{\overline{com}}_{\nu_{1}}{P_{2}}^{\overline{com}}_{\nu_{2}}{P_{3}}^{\overline{com}}_{\nu_{3}}{P_{4}}^{\overline{com}}_{\nu_{4}}\;\mathcal{T}_{13\rightarrow 24}{}_{\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{3}}^{-\lambda_{2},\lambda_{4}}(t,s,u)

(348)

and we can finally extract the crossing relation by dotting on both sides with the vector
\bar{\mathfrak{v}}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{t}}(-1,0,0,0)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)=\mathcal{T}_{13\rightarrow 24}{}_{\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{3}}^{-\lambda_{2},\lambda_{4}}(t,s,u)

(349)

For the case at hand, i.e photon scattering, the two processes are the same since all the particles
1,2,3
and
4
are identical and the above
s-t
crossing relation is a symmetry of the amplitude. Similarly one can repeat the process to establish the other crossing equations.

Appendix H
Asymptotic unitarity constraints

As described in section
3
, we impose the unitarity constraints up to some
L_{\text{max}}
and on a grid of
s
values. In this appendix we describe how we supplement them with additional constraints by analyzing the unitarity equations as
\ell\rightarrow\infty
and
s\rightarrow\infty
. In principle, these constraints should become redundant as we increase
L_{\text{max}}
and choose finer grids in
s
. In practice, we find that adding in these asymptotic constraints improves convergence in
L_{\text{max}}
and number of grid points.

H.1
Large spin

The first step is to estimate the behaviour of partial waves at large spin. To this end,
we follow the analysis of Appendix D.4 in
Paulos:2017fhb
. We would like to use the Froissart-Gribov projection formula and write partial waves as contour integrals in the complex
z=\cos\theta
plane. We define

\displaystyle e^{\,\ell}_{\lambda\mu}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{\lambda-\mu}}{2}\left[\Gamma(\ell+\lambda+1)\Gamma(\ell-\lambda+1)\Gamma(\ell+\mu+1)\Gamma(\ell-\mu+1)\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{1+z}{2}\right)^{\frac{\lambda+\mu}{2}}

(350)

\displaystyle\times\left(\frac{1-z}{2}\right)^{-\frac{\lambda-\mu}{2}}\left(\frac{z-1}{2}\right)^{-\ell-\mu-1}\frac{1}{\Gamma(2\ell+2)}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\ell+\lambda+1,\ell+\mu+1,2\ell+2,\frac{2}{1-z}\right)~,

valid for
\lambda+\mu\geq 0
and
\lambda-\mu\geq 0
. For other ranges of parameters, the function is defined by its symmetry properties

e^{\,\ell}_{\lambda\mu}(z)=(-1)^{\lambda-\mu}e^{\,\ell}_{\mu\lambda}(z)=(-1)^{\lambda-\mu}e^{\,\ell}_{-\lambda,-\mu}(z)

(351)

This function has a branch cut in the complex
z
plane between
-1
and
1
and its discontinuity there is the Wigner
d
function:
44
44

44

In this respect, it’s a generalization of the Legendre Q function, which obeys an analogous relation to the Legendre P polynomial.
45
45

45

In general, the Wigner
e
function has additional singularities at
z=\pm 1
. The leading singular behaviour there is
(z+1)^{(\lambda+\mu)/2}
and
(z-1)^{(\lambda-\mu)/2}
. In the case relevant for us, we have

\displaystyle e^{\ell}_{2,2}(z)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{2(-1)^{\ell}}{\ell(\ell+1)}\frac{1}{(z+1)}+\frac{12(-1)^{\ell-1}}{(\ell-1)\ell(\ell+1)(\ell+2)}\frac{1}{(z+1)^{2}}

(352)

\displaystyle e^{\ell}_{0,2}(z)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{2}{\sqrt{(\ell-1)\ell(\ell+1)(\ell+2)}}\frac{1}{(z+1)}+\frac{2(-1)^{\ell}}{\sqrt{(\ell-1)\ell(\ell+1)(\ell+2)}}\frac{1}{(z-1)}

e^{\,\ell}_{\lambda\mu}(z+i\epsilon)-e^{\,\ell}_{\lambda\mu}(z-i\epsilon)=-i\pi d^{\,\ell}_{\lambda\mu}(z)\qquad z\in(-1,1)

(353)

We now recall the definition of partial wave amplitudes

{\mathcal{T}^{\ell}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s)=\int_{-1}^{+1}dz\;d^{\,\ell}_{\lambda_{12}\lambda_{34}}(z)\,\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,z)

(354)

At this point we would like to use
353
to write the above equation as a contour integral in the
z
plane. Scattering amplitudes have the following behaviour near
z=\pm 1
46
46

46

see for example eq.(2.138) in
Hebbar:2020ukp
.

\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,z)=b_{\lambda_{12}\lambda_{34}}(z)\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,z),

(355)

where we have defined the
b
function

b_{\lambda\mu}(z)=\left(\frac{1+z}{2}\right)^{\frac{|\lambda+\mu|}{2}}\left(\frac{1-z}{2}\right)^{\frac{|\lambda-\mu|}{2}}

(356)

and with the function
\hat{\mathcal{T}}
being regular near
z=\pm 1
. This behaviour of the scattering amplitude precisely cancels the poles (more generally branch cuts) mentioned in footnote
45
.
We can now re-write the partial wave integral as a contour integral

{\mathcal{T}^{\ell}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s)=\frac{1}{i\pi}\oint_{C}b_{\lambda_{12}\lambda_{34}}(z)e^{\,\ell}_{\lambda_{12}\lambda_{34}}(z)\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,z)

(357)

with the contour
C
circling the line segment
[-1,1]
anti-clockwise. In fact, in the presence of massless particles, the contour should pass through
z=1
and
z=-1
, as it will be clear below.
Therefore for large enough
\ell
,
47
47

47

Note that
e_{\lambda\mu}^{\ell}\sim z^{-\ell}
for large
|z|
.
we can open up the contour and drop the arcs at infinity, to arrive at the (generalized) Froissart-Gribov projection formula

{\mathcal{T}^{\ell}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s)=\frac{1}{i\pi}\left(\int_{z_{t}}^{\infty}dz\;b_{\lambda_{12}\lambda_{34}}(z)\,e^{\,\ell}_{\lambda_{12}\lambda_{34}}(z)\text{Disc}_{t}\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,z)\right.\\
\left.+\int_{-z_{u}}^{-\infty}dz\;b_{\lambda_{12}\lambda_{34}}(z)\,e^{\,\ell}_{\lambda_{12}\lambda_{34}}(z)\text{Disc}_{u}\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,z)\right)

(358)

where the 1st term is due to the
t
channel branch cut from
[z_{t},\infty)
and the 2nd term is due to the
u
channel branch cut from
(-\infty,-z_{u}]
.

For theories with a mass gap,
z_{t}=1+\frac{2t_{0}}{s-4m^{2}}>1
and
z_{u}=1+\frac{2u_{0}}{s-4m^{2}}>1
and due to the exponential decay in spin of the
e
function, the partial wave amplitudes also have an exponential fall off in spin. For theories with massless particles this is not the case, because
z_{t}\rightarrow 1
and
z_{u}\rightarrow 1
.

We therefore consider the large
\ell
,
z\rightarrow 1^{+}
limit of the hypergeometric function. Assuming
\lambda\ll\ell
,
\mu\ll\ell
we find

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\ell+\lambda+1,\ell+\mu+1,2\ell+2,\frac{2}{1-z}\right)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{2\,\Gamma(2\ell+2)}{\Gamma(\ell+\mu+1)\Gamma(\ell-\mu+1)}\left(\frac{z-1}{2}\right)^{\ell+1+\frac{\mu+\lambda}{2}}

(359)

\displaystyle\times\,K_{\lambda-\mu}\left(\sqrt{2(z-1)}\,\ell\right).

Hence we obtain an approximation for the
e
function

\displaystyle e^{\,\ell}_{\lambda\mu}(z)

\displaystyle\approx(-1)^{\frac{\lambda-\mu}{2}}K_{\lambda-\mu}\left(\sqrt{2(z-1)}\,\ell\right),

(360)

valid for
\ell\gg 1
and
z\rightarrow 1^{+}
and
\lambda\ll\ell
,
\mu\ll\ell
.
For the other limit
\ell\gg 1
and
z\rightarrow-1^{-}
, we use the relation

e^{\,\ell}_{\lambda\mu}(-z)=(-1)^{l-\lambda+1}e^{\,\ell}_{\lambda,-\mu}(z)

(361)

The other piece in (
358
) is the discontinuity of the amplitude
\hat{T}\equiv\frac{T}{b}
. In our numerics we parametrize the amplitude as displayed in (
46
). The prefactors, composed of
\chi_{s}
,
\chi_{t}
and
\chi_{u}
, which were added for kinematical reasons, ensure the cancellation of the singularities at
z=\pm 1
. We therefore consider the
\rho
series:

\sum_{abc}\alpha_{abc}\rho^{a}(s)\rho^{b}(t)\rho^{c}(u)

(362)

For such a series, the
t
-channel discontinuity comes from

\rho(t(s,z+i\epsilon))^{b}-\rho(t(s,z-i\epsilon))^{b}\approx 2ib\sqrt{2s}\sqrt{z-1}

(363)

and the
u
-channel discontinuity comes from

\rho(u(s,z-i\epsilon))^{c}-\rho(u(s,z+i\epsilon))^{c}\approx 2ic\sqrt{2s}\sqrt{-z-1}

(364)

By making a change of variable
z\rightarrow-z
for the
u
channel contribution and using the symmetry properties (
361
) of the
e
function and the
b
function

b_{\lambda\mu}(-z)=b_{\lambda,-\mu}(z)

(365)

we can reduce all our computations to that of integrals of the following form:

\int_{1}^{\infty}dz\left(1+z\right)^{m}\left(1-z\right)^{n}\sqrt{z-1}K_{a}\left(c\sqrt{z-1}\right)

(366)

To perform this integral we make a change of variable
(z-1)=\xi^{2}
and then expand in the variable
\xi
to get the following type of integrals which are easily evaluated

\int_{0}^{\infty}d\xi\,\xi^{b}\,K_{a}\left(c\,\xi\right)=\frac{2^{b-1}}{c^{b+1}}\Gamma\left(\frac{b-a+1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{b+a+1}{2}\right)

(367)

Since
c\sim\ell
, the leading contribution comes from the lowest order term in the
\xi
expansion.
At leading order in the large
\ell
limit, we have
48
48

48

Recall that due to crossing symmetry
\Phi_{3}(s,t,u)=\Phi_{1}(u,t,s)
, which is why the same coefficients
\alpha^{(1)}_{abc}
appear in the expansion for both the amplitudes, albeit with different orderings of the
a
,
b
and
c
indices.

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}^{\ell}\equiv{\mathcal{T}^{\ell}}_{+,+}^{+,+}(s)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{\ell^{3}}\sum_{abc}\alpha^{(1)}_{abc}\left(b\,\rho^{c}(-s)+(-1)^{\ell}c\,\rho^{b}(-s)\right)\sqrt{s}\,\rho^{a}(s)\chi^{2}(s),

(368)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}^{\ell}\equiv{\mathcal{T}^{\ell}}_{+,+}^{-,-}(s)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{\ell^{3}}\sum_{abc}\alpha^{(2)}_{abc}\left(b\,\rho^{c}(-s)+(-1)^{\ell}c\,\rho^{b}(-s)\right)\sqrt{s}\,\rho^{a}(s),

\displaystyle\Phi_{3}^{\ell}\equiv{\mathcal{T}^{\ell}}_{+,-}^{+,-}(s)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{\ell^{3}}\sum_{abc}\alpha^{(1)}_{cba}b\,\rho^{c}(-s)\sqrt{s}\,\rho^{a}(s)\chi^{2}(-s),

\displaystyle\Phi_{5}^{\ell}\equiv{\mathcal{T}^{\ell}}_{+,+}^{+,-}(s)

\displaystyle\approx\mathcal{O}(l^{-4}).

We notice that the fifth amplitude
\Phi_{5}^{\ell}
is sub-leading at large spin and therefore the unitarity condition (
139
) decomposes into two
1\times 1
conditions

\displaystyle|1+i(\Phi_{1}^{\ell}+\Phi_{2}^{\ell})|\leq 1,

(369)

\displaystyle|1+i(2\Phi_{3}^{\ell})|\leq 1.

In addition we have from (
136
)

|1+i(\Phi_{1}^{\ell}-\Phi_{2}^{\ell})|\leq 1.\\

(370)

Plugging in (
368
) into the above unitarity equations we get the following large spin conditions:
